\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}^{-1}(t,t_{0})=e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\lambda_{\alpha}(\xi)d\xi}\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{m_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\tilde{u}_{n_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{n_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{m_{\alpha}}(t)|\text{ , }

(137)

where the coefficients should satisfies

\sum_{j_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}u_{n_{\nu}j_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\nu}m_{\nu}}=\delta_{n_{\nu}m_{\nu}}\text{ . }

(138)

In addition, the operator
\pazocal{U}(t,t_{0})
can be designed in order to be identified as the operator which “block-diagonalizes”
\mathbbm{L}(t)
, that is, if we impose four additional conditions given by

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{l_{\nu}(n_{\nu}-1)}u_{n_{\nu}l_{\nu}}=0

\displaystyle\quad\text{ , }\quad\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{l_{\nu}n_{\nu}}u_{n_{\nu}l_{\nu}}=1\text{ , }

(139a)

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{l_{\nu}n_{\nu}}u_{n_{\nu}(l_{\nu}+1)}=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{ and }\quad\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{l_{\nu}(n_{\nu}-1)}u_{n_{\nu}(l_{\nu}+1)}=1\text{ , }

(139b)

so one can show that

\mathbbm{L}_{\text{J}}(t)=\pazocal{U}^{-1}(t,t_{0})\mathbbm{L}(t)\pazocal{U}(t,t_{0})\text{ . }

(140)

A detailed proof of Eqs. (
138
) and (
139
) is provided in Appendix
8
. In particular, from Eqs. (
134
) and (
114
) we can redo the above analysis in order to show that the adiabatic evolution operator for one-dimensional Jordan blocks reads

\pazocal{U}^{1\text{D}}(t,t_{0})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\Lambda_{\alpha}(\xi)d\xi}|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}(t)\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}(t_{0})|\text{ . }

(141)

In fact, by starting the system in an arbitrary initial state
|\rho(t_{0})\rangle\rangle=r_{\alpha}(t_{0})|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle
, one shows that the solution
|\rho^{1\text{D}}_{\text{ad}}(t)\rangle\rangle
given in Eq. (
114
) can be obtained from above equation as
|\rho^{1\text{D}}_{\text{ad}}(t)\rangle\rangle=\pazocal{U}^{1\text{D}}(t,t_{0})|\rho(t_{0})\rangle\rangle
. Moreover, as we shall see, the knowledge of the evolution operator for adiabatic dynamics is the main element required to study transitionless quantum driving in open systems. For this reason, the evolution operators derived in this section are important results of this thesis.

9
Application to adiabatic Deutsch algorithm

For the applications of adiabatic approximation in open system considered in this thesis, the system dynamics is given by

\dot{\rho}(t)=\pazocal{H}[\rho(t)]+\pazocal{R}[\rho(t)]\text{ , }

(142)

with
\pazocal{H}[\bullet]
is the unitary part of the dynamics given by

\pazocal{H}[\bullet]=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[H(t),\bullet]\text{ , }

(143)

and
\pazocal{R}[\bullet]
is the non-unitary contribution to the dynamics. In particular, here we will be interested in a number of evolution given by Lindblad master equation
[
24
]

\pazocal{R}[\rho(t)]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n}\left[2\Gamma_{n}(t)\rho(t)\Gamma^{\dagger}_{n}(t)-\left\{\Gamma^{\dagger}_{n}(t)\Gamma_{n}(t),\rho(t)\right\}\right]\text{ , }

(144)

where
\Gamma_{n}(t)
are the Lindblad operators.

9.1
Adiabatic Deutsch algorithm under dephasing

At beginning of the development of quantum computation the main question was finding some concrete example (some algorithm) in which quantum mechanically driven system could provide computation tasks with high performance concerning its classical counterpart. In this sense, in 1985, David Deutsch derived a quantum algorithm in which we can get a enhanced performance from its classical one
[
122
]
, called
Deutsch algorithm
. Although the Deutsch’s algorithm is not a important (powerful) algorithm in quantum computation, since it does not provide a exponential gain concerning the its classical version, it is the first main example of how powerful a quantum algorithm can be. The problem addressed in Deutsch’s algorithm is how to determinate whether a dichotomic real function
f:x\in\{0,1\}\rightarrow f(x)\in\{0,1\}
is
constant
(the output result
f(x)
is the same regardless the input value
x
) or
balanced
(the output result
f(x)
assumes different values in according with the input value
x
). The adiabatic version of such algorithm is obtained through the operator
\pazocal{O}_{f}
which computes
f
(oracle), given by

\pazocal{O}_{f}=(-1)^{f(0)}|0\rangle\langle 0|+(-1)^{f(1)}|1\rangle\langle 1|\text{ , }

(145)

thus one can write the adiabatic Hamiltonian which implements the algorithm as

H^{\text{DA}}(t)=U_{f}(t)H_{0}U_{f}^{\dagger}(t)\text{ , }

(146)

where
H_{0}=-\hbar\omega\sigma_{x}/2
and
U_{f}(t)=\exp(i\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\pazocal{O}_{f})
. At
t=0
we have
H^{\text{DA}}(0)=H_{0}
, so that the initial input state is written as
|\psi_{\text{inp}}\rangle=|+\rangle=(1/\sqrt{2})(|0\rangle+|1\rangle)
. One can show that, when the evolution is slow enough, the adiabatic dynamics of the system driven by the Hamiltonian
H^{\text{DA}}(t)
will be given by

\rho_{\text{cs}}^{\text{DA}}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathbbm{1}+\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\sigma_{x}-\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\sigma_{y}\right]\text{ , }

(147)

where
F=1-(-1)^{f(0)+f(1)}
and the subscript “cs” denotes that
\rho_{\text{cs}}^{\text{DA}}(t)
is obtained from adiabatic solution for closed systems. By considering the system has its dynamics governed by an environment so that the main effects on the system is phase damping with rate
\gamma(t)
, whose contribution reads

\pazocal{R}[\bullet]=\gamma(t)\left[\sigma_{z}\bullet\sigma_{z}-\bullet\right]

(148)

we describe the system evolution as

\dot{\rho}^{\text{DA}}(t)=-\frac{i}{\hbar}[H^{\text{DA}}(t),\rho^{\text{DA}}(t)]+\gamma(t)\left[\sigma_{z}\rho^{\text{DA}}(t)\sigma_{z}-\rho^{\text{DA}}(t)\right]\text{ . }

(149)

In order to study the adiabatic dynamics of the system in according with above equation, let us to rewrite it in superoperator formalism as

|\dot{\rho}^{\text{DA}}(t)\rangle\rangle=\mathbbm{L}^{\text{DA}}(t)|\rho^{\text{DA}}(t)\rangle\rangle\text{ , }

(150)

where

\mathbbm{L}^{\text{DA}}(t)=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0&0\\
0&-2\gamma&0&\omega\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\\
0&0&-2\gamma&\omega\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\\
0&-\omega\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)&-\omega\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)&0\end{bmatrix}\text{ , }

(151)

whose the eigenvectors are (the superscript “t” denotes transpose)

\displaystyle|\pazocal{D}^{\text{DA}}_{0}(t)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{}{}1\quad 0\quad 0\quad 0\text{ }\right]^{\text{t}}\text{ , }

(152a)

\displaystyle|\pazocal{D}^{\text{DA}}_{1}(t)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\left[\text{ }0\quad-\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad 0\text{ }\right]^{\text{t}}\text{ , }

(152b)

\displaystyle|\pazocal{D}^{\text{DA}}_{2}(t)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\left[\text{ }0\quad\Delta_{+}(t)\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad\Delta_{+}(t)\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad 1\text{ }\right]^{\text{t}}\text{ , }

(152c)

\displaystyle|\pazocal{D}^{\text{DA}}_{3}(t)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\left[\text{ }0\quad\Delta^{-1}_{+}(t)\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad\Delta^{-1}_{+}(t)\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad 1\text{ }\right]^{\text{t}}\text{ , }

(152d)

and left eigenvectors

\displaystyle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}^{\text{DA}}_{0}(t)|

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{}{}1\quad 0\quad 0\quad 0\text{ }\right]\text{ , }

(153a)

\displaystyle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}^{\text{DA}}_{1}(t)|

\displaystyle=\left[\text{ }0\quad-\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad 0\text{ }\right]\text{ , }

(153b)

\displaystyle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}^{\text{DA}}_{2}(t)|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\text{ }0\quad\omega\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad\omega\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad-\frac{\Delta_{-}(t)}{\sqrt{\gamma^{2}(t)-\omega^{2}}}\text{ }\right]\text{ , }

(153c)

\displaystyle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}^{\text{DA}}_{3}(t)|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[\text{ }0\quad-\omega\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad-\omega\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad\frac{\Delta_{+}(t)}{\sqrt{\gamma^{2}(t)-\omega^{2}}}\text{ }\right]\text{ , }

(153d)

with eigenvalues
\lambda_{0}(t)=0
,
\lambda_{1}(t)=-2\gamma(t)
,
\lambda_{2}(t)=-\Delta_{+}(t)
and
\lambda_{3}(t)=-\Delta_{-}(t)
, where we defined
\Delta_{\pm}(t)=\gamma(t)\pm\sqrt{\gamma^{2}(t)-\omega^{2}}
. The non-degenerate spectrum of
\mathbbm{L}^{\text{DA}}(t)
shows that it can be written in one-dimensional Jordan block form, so that the adiabatic behavior of the system can be obtained from adiabatic solution discussed in Sec.
8.1
. By writing the density matrix of the initial state
\rho^{\text{DA}}(0)=|\psi_{\text{inp}}\rangle\langle\psi_{\text{inp}}|=|+\rangle\langle+|=(1/2)(\mathbbm{1}+\sigma_{x})
in superoperator formalism, one shows that the initial state can be written as a linear combination of the vectors
|\pazocal{D}^{\text{DA}}_{0}(0)\rangle\rangle
and
|\pazocal{D}^{\text{DA}}_{1}(0)\rangle\rangle
as

|\rho^{\text{DA}}(0)\rangle\rangle=\begin{bmatrix}\text{ }1&1&0&0\text{ }\end{bmatrix}^{\text{t}}=|\pazocal{D}^{\text{DA}}_{0}(0)\rangle\rangle-|\pazocal{D}^{\text{DA}}_{1}(0)\rangle\rangle\text{ . }

(154)

Hence, if we let the system evolves adiabatically, the evolved state is obtained from Eq. (
114
) as

|\rho^{\text{DA}}(t)\rangle\rangle=|\pazocal{D}^{\text{DA}}_{0}(t)\rangle\rangle-e^{-2\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\gamma(\xi)d\xi}|\pazocal{D}^{\text{DA}}_{1}(t)\rangle\rangle\text{ , }

(155)

where we already used that
\Lambda_{1}(t)=\lambda_{1}(t)=-2\gamma(t)
and
\Lambda_{0}(t)=\lambda_{0}(t)=0
, since from Eqs. (
152
) and (
153
) one can see that
\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{0}(t)|\dot{\pazocal{D}}_{0}(t)\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{1}(t)|\dot{\pazocal{D}}_{1}(t)\rangle\rangle=0
. Now, by rewrite the above equation in matrix notation we get

|\rho^{\text{DA}}(t)\rangle\rangle=\left[\text{ }1\quad-e^{-2\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\gamma(\xi)d\xi}\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad-e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\lambda_{1}(\xi)d\xi}\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\quad 0\text{ }\right]^{\text{t}}\text{ , }

(156)

where we can easily to determinate the components of the coherence vector associated with density matrix
\rho^{\text{DA}}(t)
and write

\rho^{\text{DA}}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathbbm{1}+e^{-2\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\gamma(\xi)d\xi}\cos\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\sigma_{x}-e^{-2\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\gamma(\xi)d\xi}\sin\left(\frac{\pi F}{2}\frac{t}{\tau}\right)\sigma_{y}\right]\text{ . }

(157)

In unitary dynamics limit
\gamma(t)\rightarrow 0
, it is straightforward to see that we recover the density matrix of the unitary dynamics given in Eq. (
147
). However, the above solution is just valid when the adiabatic regime is achieved, in other words, when the adiabaticity conditions discussed in Sec.
8
. In this case more precisely, the relevant conditions are related with one-dimensional Jordan blocks as discussed in Sec.
8.1
. Thus, by defining
\mathcal{G}_{\beta\alpha}(s)=\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}(s)|d_{s}\pazocal{D}_{\beta}(s)\rangle\rangle
, where we are using
s=t/\tau
, we find the non-null elements given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{12}(s)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi F\Delta_{+}(s)}{2\omega}\text{ \, \, , \, \, }\mathcal{G}_{13}(s)=-\frac{\pi F\omega}{2\Delta_{+}(s)}\text{ , }

(158a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{21}(s)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{G}_{31}(s)=\frac{\pi F\omega}{4\sqrt{\gamma^{2}(s)-\omega^{2}}}\text{ , }

(158b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{22}(s)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{G}_{32}(s)=\frac{\Delta_{+}(s)d_{s}\gamma(s)}{2\left(\gamma^{2}(s)-\omega^{2}\right)}\text{ , }

(158c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{23}(s)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{G}_{33}(s)=-\frac{\omega^{2}d_{s}\gamma(s)}{2\left(\gamma^{2}(s)-\omega^{2}\right)\Delta_{+}(s)}\text{ , }

(158d)

with
\Delta_{\pm}(s)=\gamma(s)\pm\sqrt{\gamma^{2}(s)-\omega^{2}}
. From above equation, it is possible to see that we can simplify our discussion if we consider
\gamma(t)=\gamma(s)=\gamma_{0}
, so that
\mathcal{G}_{22}(s)=\mathcal{G}_{32}(s)=\mathcal{G}_{23}(s)=\mathcal{G}_{33}(s)=0
, moreover, the rest of the above quantities are time-independent by using this assumption. Thus, from Eqs. (
125
) we have the adiabaticity coefficients given by

\displaystyle\Xi_{12}^{(1)}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-s\tau\gamma_{0}}F\pi\left|\omega p_{1}(s)\right|}{4\tau\left|\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}\left(\gamma_{0}-i\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}^{2}}\right)\right|}\text{ \, \, , \, \, }\Xi_{13}^{(1)}(s)=\frac{e^{-s\tau\gamma_{0}}F\pi\left|\omega p_{1}(s)\right|}{4\tau\left|\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}\left(\gamma_{0}+i\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}^{2}}\right)\right|}\text{ , }

(159a)

\displaystyle\Xi_{21}^{(1)}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{s\gamma_{0}\tau}F\pi\left|\omega p_{3}(s)\right|}{2\tau\left|\gamma_{0}+i\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}^{2}}\right|}\text{ \, \, , \, \, }\Xi_{31}^{(1)}(s)=\frac{e^{s\gamma_{0}\tau}F\pi|p_{2}(s)|}{2\omega^{3}\tau}\left|\omega^{2}-2\gamma_{0}\left(\gamma_{0}+i\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}^{2}}\right)\right|\text{ , }

(159b)

where we already used that
\omega\geq\gamma
, so that
\left|\text{exp}\sqrt{\gamma_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}}\right|=1
. To end, the second adiabatic coefficients are

\displaystyle\Xi_{12}^{(2)}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-s\tau\gamma_{0}}F\pi\left|\omega d_{s}p_{1}(s)\right|}{4\tau\left|\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}\left(\gamma_{0}-i\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}^{2}}\right)\right|}\text{ \, \, , \, \, }\Xi_{13}^{(2)}(s)=\frac{e^{-s\tau\gamma_{0}}F\pi\left|\omega d_{s}p_{1}(s)\right|}{4\tau\left|\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}\left(\gamma_{0}+i\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}^{2}}\right)\right|}\text{ , }

(160a)

\displaystyle\Xi_{21}^{(2)}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{s\gamma_{0}\tau}F\pi\left|\omega d_{s}p_{3}(s)\right|}{2\tau\left|\gamma_{0}+i\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}^{2}}\right|}\text{ \, \, , \, \, }\Xi_{31}^{(2)}(s)=\frac{e^{s\gamma_{0}\tau}F\pi|d_{s}p_{2}(s)|}{2\omega^{3}\tau}\left|\omega^{2}-2\gamma_{0}\left(\gamma_{0}+i\sqrt{\omega^{2}-\gamma_{0}^{2}}\right)\right|\text{ . }

(160b)

The other coefficients are zero and this means that some Lindblad-Jordan eigenspaces are uncoupled between each other for any
\tau>0
. In fact, we found that
\Xi_{\alpha 0}^{(1)}(s)=\Xi_{\alpha 0}^{(2)}(s)=0
so that the eigenvector
|\pazocal{D}_{0}(s)\rangle\rangle
evolves independent on the
\alpha
-th eigenvector of
\mathbbm{L}(s)
. On the other hand,
\Xi_{0\alpha}^{(1)}(s)=\Xi_{0\alpha}^{(2)}(s)=0
means that the
\alpha
-th eigenvector of
\mathbbm{L}(s)
evolves independent on the eigenvector
|\pazocal{D}_{0}(s)\rangle\rangle
.

In Eqs. (
159
), due to the positive value of the exponential in above equations, the value of
\Xi_{21}^{(1)}(s)
and
\Xi_{31}^{(1)}(s)
should increase as
\gamma_{0}\tau\rightarrow\infty
, however in some cases there is a critical values
\Xi_{21}^{(1)}(s)
and
\Xi_{31}^{(1)}(s)
for some values of
\gamma_{0}\tau
. Therefore, as discussed in Ref.
[
25
]
, for a range of values for
\gamma_{0}\tau
the adiabatic behavior is approximately obtained, but as
\gamma_{0}\tau
grows the coefficients
\Xi_{21}^{(1)}(s)
and
\Xi_{31}^{(1)}(s)
become big enough so that we achieve a regime in which an
adiabaticity breakdown
happens. On the other hand, let us consider a more robust analysis by studying the behavior of the other coefficients
\Xi_{12}^{(1)}(s)
and
\Xi_{13}^{(1)}(s)
. The negative exponential in
\Xi_{12}^{(1)}(s)
and
\Xi_{13}^{(1)}(s)
shows a different behavior because as
\tau
increases the coefficient
e^{-s\gamma_{0}\tau}/\gamma_{0}\tau
decreases. Moreover, in the limit
\gamma_{0}\tau\rightarrow\infty
, one gets
\Xi_{12}^{(1)}(s)\rightarrow 0
and
\Xi_{13}^{(1)}(s)\rightarrow 0
. The same result is obtained for the coefficients in Eqs. (
160
). From this analysis, we have a scenario where in the limit of slow dynamics (
\gamma_{0}\tau\gg 1
) the eigenvector
|\pazocal{D}_{1}(s)\rangle\rangle
does not evolve uncoupled from
|\pazocal{D}_{2}(s)\rangle\rangle
and
|\pazocal{D}_{3}(s)\rangle\rangle
(no adiabaticity), but the eigenvectors
|\pazocal{D}_{2}(s)\rangle\rangle
and
|\pazocal{D}_{3}(s)\rangle\rangle
are decoupled from
|\pazocal{D}_{1}(s)\rangle\rangle
. However, interestingly, here we will discuss how it is possible to proof that adiabaticity can be obtained in the regime
\gamma_{0}\tau\gg 1
.

By using the Eqs. (
158
), from set of equations given in Eq. (
109
), one finds
\dot{r}_{0}(t)=0
and

\displaystyle d_{s}r_{1}(s)

\displaystyle=\tau\lambda_{1}(s)r_{1}(s)-r_{2}(s)\mathcal{G}_{12}(s)-r_{3}(s)\mathcal{G}_{13}(s)\text{ , }

(161a)

\displaystyle d_{s}r_{2}(s)

\displaystyle=\tau\lambda_{2}(s)r_{2}(s)-r_{1}(s)\mathcal{G}_{21}(s)\text{ , }

(161b)

\displaystyle d_{s}r_{3}(s)

\displaystyle=\tau\lambda_{3}(s)r_{3}(s)-r_{1}(s)\mathcal{G}_{31}(s)\text{ . }

(161c)

Now, we use the discussion above in order to rewrite the Eqs. (
161b
) and (
161c
) in adiabatic regime as (
j=2,3
)

d_{s}r_{j}(s)\approx\tau\lambda_{j}(s)r_{j}(s)\text{ \, \, \, }\Rightarrow\text{ \, \, \, }r_{j}(s)=r_{j}(0)e^{\tau\int_{0}^{s}\lambda_{j}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}}\text{ . }

(162)

As it was already discussed here, the coefficients
r_{2}(s)
and
r_{3}(s)
do not depend on the other coefficients. However, by rewriting the Eq. (
161a
) in this regime we get

d_{s}r_{1}(s)=\tau\lambda_{1}(s)r_{1}(s)-r_{2}(0)e^{\tau\int_{0}^{s}\lambda_{2}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{12}(s)-r_{3}(0)e^{\tau\int_{0}^{s}\lambda_{3}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}}\mathcal{G}_{13}(s)\text{ , }

(163)

thus, note that if we consider the initial state such that
r_{2}(0)=r_{3}(0)=0
, then we find

d_{s}r_{1}(s)|_{r_{j}(0)=0}=\tau\lambda_{1}(s)r_{1}(s)\text{ \, \, \, }\Rightarrow\text{ \, \, \, }r_{1}(s)|_{r_{j}(0)=0}=r_{1}(0)e^{\int_{0}^{s}\tau\lambda_{1}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}}\text{ , }

(164)

where the adiabatic dynamics is obtained. From this example we conclude that the adiabatic approximation in open system requires a more detailed study, we mean, the validity conditions of the adiabatic dynamics alone are not enough to guarantee the adiabatic behavior in open systems. In conclusion, this discussion allows us to guarantee that the Deutsch algorithm considered here achieves its adiabatic dynamics when very-slowly driven fields are used. In fact, from Eq. (
154
) it is possible to see that the initial condition
r_{2}(0)=r_{3}(0)=0
is satisfied and the dynamics is dictated by the Eq. (
155
).

9.2
Experimental verification and asymptotic adiabatic dynamics for open systems

In order to verify the theoretical results obtained in last section, where the adiabatic dynamics in open system is obtained in asymptotic long evolution total time, in this section we will present the experimental verification in a trapped ion Ytterbium qubit
[
123
]
. However, different from Sec.
4.2
where the dynamics is unitary, here we need to consider the non-unitary effects on the dynamics because of the long time coherence of the system
[
124
]
. In fact, as shown in Appendix
7.A.4
, while the Rabi frequency achieved in our experiment is of the order of KHz, the natural dephasing rate
\gamma_{\text{nd}}
of the system used in our experiment was computed as
\gamma_{\text{nd}}\approx 3.03
Hz. For this reason, a simulation of non-unitary effects on the two-level system is required and it is done with the experimental apparatus shown in Fig.
10
. For more details about the feature used in the simulation, see Appendix
4.2
.

Figure 10:
A modified version of the experimental setup presented in Fig.
2
a
, where here we have a new electronic component used to introduce a Gaussian noise in driving microwave.

As a quantifier of the adiabatic dynamics we define the fidelity between two density matrices
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
given by

\pazocal{F}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})=\text{Tr}\sqrt{\sqrt{\rho_{1}}\rho_{2}\sqrt{\rho_{1}}}\text{ , }

(165)

from what we can set here two important fidelities. By putting
\rho_{1}
as the desired dynamics and
\rho_{2}
as the numerical solution of the dynamics, we define the theoretical fidelity
\pazocal{F}_{\text{the}}(t)
. Otherwise, when
\rho_{2}
assume the values of the experimental density matrix (obtained from tomography process) we have the experimental fidelity
\pazocal{F}_{\text{exp}}(t)
. Said that, the theoretical/experimental fidelity of the system follows an adiabatic path in open system as function of the total evolution time
\tau
for the Deutsch algorithm reads

\pazocal{F}_{\text{the(exp)}}(\tau)=\text{Tr}\sqrt{\sqrt{\rho^{\text{DA}}(\tau)}\rho_{\text{the(exp)}}(\tau)\sqrt{\rho^{\text{DA}}(\tau)}}\text{ , }

(166)

with
\rho_{\text{the(exp)}}(\tau)
being obtained through numerical solution (experimental realization) and
\rho^{\text{DA}}(\tau)
is given by Eq. (
157
). In case where
\pazocal{F}_{\text{the(exp)}}(\tau)\approx 1
, we have the adiabatic solution with high fidelity, otherwise the dynamics is not adiabatic. For the experiment of the Deutsch algorithm we take a balanced function as example and we set the parameter
\omega=2\pi\times 10
KHz, so that in Fig.
11
we present the results for the fidelity
\pazocal{F}(\tau)
. In all of the experiments realized in this work, the error bars are obtained from the standard deviation associated with 60 000 repetitions of the experiment. For every fidelity, we perform state tomography by measuring the qubit in the three Pauli bases (
\sigma_{x}
,
\sigma_{y}
, and
\sigma_{z}
)
[
125
]
, with every basis measured
2000
times and repeated 10 times.

Figure 11:
(
a
) Fidelity for the adiabatic open system behavior of the Deutsch algorithm. It is shown
\pazocal{F}^{\text{os}}_{\text{the}}(\tau)
(continuum lines) and its experimental counterpart
\pazocal{F}^{\text{os}}_{\text{exp}}(\tau)
(symbols) as functions of
\tau
for several values of the dephasing rate
\gamma
. (
b
) Fidelity for the pure target state in the Deutsch algorithm for several values of the dephasing rate
\gamma
. Lines represent
\pazocal{F}^{\text{cs}}_{\text{the}}(\tau)
and the dots its experimental counterpart
\pazocal{F}^{\text{cs}}_{\text{exp}}(\tau)
. In both graphs we consider a balanced function with
\omega=2\pi\times 10
KHz. Due the high controllability and measurement fidelity, some experimental points data are superposed.

It is argued in Ref.
[
25
]
that there should be an optimal time for the adiabatic approximation. While this is true in general, here fidelity increases to its maximum as
\tau\rightarrow\infty
due to the initial conditions of the system state. Actually, as discussed in previous section, the relation between adiabatic and the decoherence time scales allows us to get an adiabatic dynamics for very-slowly driven Lindbladian. It is also important to stress here that the high fidelity for the adiabatic behavior as
\tau\rightarrow\infty
does not imply in a high fidelity for the target state of the algorithm. Indeed, the output for a balanced function
f
is the pure state
|-\rangle
, which can be represented by the density operator
\rho_{\text{cs}}^{\text{DA}}(\tau)
given in Eq. (
147
). In order to quantify the success of the algorithm under decoherence, we define the fidelity of the real dynamics with respect to the target output density operator given by

\pazocal{F}^{\text{cs}}_{\text{exp}}(\tau)=\text{Tr}\sqrt{\sqrt{\rho_{\text{cs}}^{\text{DA}}(\tau)}\rho_{\text{the(exp)}}(t)\sqrt{\rho_{\text{cs}}^{\text{DA}}(\tau)}}\text{ . }

(167)

Observe that, since we are evolving under decoherence, the target state
\rho_{\text{cs}}^{\text{DA}}(\tau)
is distinct of the adiabatic solution given in Eq. (
157
), with the adiabatic solution reducing to the target state in the limit of vanishing decoherence. The fidelity with respect to the pure target state in Fig.
11
b
decays for long times as the decoherence rate increases. This is in contrast with the fidelity with respect to the mixed-state adiabatic evolution for open system in Fig.
11
a
, which goes to one as
t\rightarrow\infty
. This is because adiabaticity in open systems is related to the decoupling of Jordan blocks in a nonunitary evolution, which is only approximately equivalent (in the weak coupling regime) to achieving a pure target state after a computation process. Therefore, by focusing on pure target states, it is possible to see a preferred time-window exhibiting maximum fidelity. In Fig.
11
a
, we show two windows with high fidelities, which are highlighted in light green color. Notice that these windows also correspond to high fidelities in Fig.
11
b
, which means that adiabaticity in open systems is indeed able to provide the target state of the Deutsch algorithm with high fidelity for convenient measurement times. Therefore, this allows us to understand a bit more how to obtain a favorable trade-off between the necessary time to achieve the adiabatic regime and the (restrictive) time scale of decohering effects.

Now, let us to generalize the above result for a more general situation. By using the master equation, it follows that the superoperator
\pazocal{L}_{t}[\bullet]
satisfies
\text{Tr}[\pazocal{L}_{t}[\rho(t)]]=0
, since
\text{Tr}[\dot{\rho}(t)]=0
.
Thus, if we consider the basis
\{\sigma_{n}\}
, with
\sigma_{0}=\mathbbm{1}
, the first row of the matrix representation of
\mathbbm{L}(t)
is vanishing.
In fact, by adopting that the matrix elements of
\mathbbm{L}(t)
are written as
\mathbbm{L}_{mn}(t)=(1/D)\text{Tr}[\sigma_{m}^{\dagger}\pazocal{L}_{t}[\sigma^{n}]]
, we have

\mathbbm{L}_{0n}(t)=\frac{1}{D}\text{Tr}[\pazocal{L}_{t}[\sigma^{n}]]=0\text{ . }

(168)

Then
\mathbbm{L}(t)
has at least one eigenvalue zero with eigenvector constant. We further assume that
\mathbbm{L}(t)
admits one-dimensional Jordan decomposition and
that there are no eigenvalue crossings in the spectrum of
\mathbbm{L}(t)
.
If we suitably order the basis so that the first eigenvalue is
\lambda_{0}=0
, we get the associated eigenvector as

|\pazocal{D}_{0}\rangle\rangle=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0&\cdots&0&0\end{bmatrix}^{t}\text{ . }

(169)

As an immediate result, it follows that
\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{0}|=|\pazocal{D}_{0}\rangle\rangle^{t}
. In addition, such vector is associated with the maximally mixed state
(1/D)\mathbbm{1}
. In fact, the elements
\varrho_{n}(t)
of
|\rho(t)\rangle\rangle
are given by
\varrho_{n}(t)=\text{Tr}[\sigma_{n}^{\dagger}\rho(t)]
, so that in the basis
\{\mathbbm{1},\sigma_{n}\}
we have

\varrho_{n}(t)=\frac{1}{D}\text{Tr}[\sigma_{k}^{\dagger}\mathbbm{1}]=\delta_{0n}\text{ . }

(170)

where we use that
\text{Tr}[\sigma_{k}^{\dagger}\sigma_{n}]=D\delta_{kn}
and
\text{Tr}[\sigma_{n}]=D\delta_{n0}
. Therefore, by writing the density matrix as in Eq. (
13
), it is possible to conclude that any physical initial state
|\rho(0)\rangle\rangle

must
be written as a combination of
|\pazocal{D}_{0}\rangle\rangle
and other vectors
|\pazocal{D}_{\beta\neq 0}(t)\rangle\rangle
. Hence, the initial state can be generally written as

|\rho(0)\rangle\rangle=c_{0}(0)|\pazocal{D}_{0}\rangle\rangle+\sum\nolimits_{\beta\neq 0}c_{\beta}(0)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}(0)\rangle\rangle\text{ , }

(171)

where
c_{0}(0)=1
and
c_{\beta}(0)
are general complex coefficients.

The dynamics of the vanishing eigenvalue subspace can be studied from equation

\dot{c}_{0}(t)=\sum_{\beta\neq 0}c_{\beta}(t)\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{0}|\dot{\pazocal{D}}_{\beta}(t)\rangle\rangle e^{\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{\beta}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}\text{ . }

(172)

where we already used
\lambda_{0}(t)=0
and
\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\beta}(t)|\dot{\pazocal{D}}_{0}(t)\rangle\rangle=0
. Now, using that the supervectors
|\dot{\pazocal{D}}_{\beta}(t)\rangle\rangle
and
\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\beta}(t)|
satisfy
\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}(t)|{\mathcal{D}}_{\beta}(t)\rangle\rangle=\delta_{\beta\alpha}
for any
\beta
and
\alpha
, we have

\frac{d}{dt}\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}(t)\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle\dot{\pazocal{E}}_{\alpha}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}(t)\rangle\rangle+\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}(t)|\dot{\pazocal{D}}_{\beta}(t)\rangle\rangle=0\text{ , }

and consequently we conclude that

\langle\langle\dot{\pazocal{E}}_{\alpha}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}(t)\rangle\rangle=-\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}(t)|\dot{\pazocal{D}}_{\beta}(t)\rangle\rangle\text{ . }

(173)

By using this result in Eq. (
172
), we get
\dot{c}_{0}(t)=0
, which then implies in
c_{0}(t)=1,\,\forall t
.

Concerning the dynamics of the remaining eigenstates of
\mathbbm{L}(t)
, let us assume that
the initial state is such that a single Jordan block is populated in addition to the block associated with
\lambda_{0}(t)
,
i.e.,
c_{\eta}(0)\neq 0
for a single
\eta\in\left[1,D^{2}-1\right]
.
We then start by looking at the adiabatic condition in Eqs. (
129
), they tell us whether or not

\beta
can evolve decoupled from
\alpha
, but it does not provide any information whether
\alpha
can evolve decoupled from
\beta
.
Assume that the adiabaticity conditions hold asymptotically in time (
t\rightarrow\infty
),
due to the fact that
\Re\left[\lambda_{\alpha}(t)-\lambda_{\beta}(t)\right]<0,\,\forall\alpha,t
.
Provided the absence of level crossings as a function of time,
this condition selects the largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{\beta}(t)
.
Then, we write
\dot{c}_{\beta}(t)=b(t)c_{\beta}(t)
, with
b(t)
denoting a complex coefficient. The solution reads
c_{\beta}(t)=c_{\beta}(0)e^{\int_{0}^{t}b(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}
.
On the other hand, once the parameter
\xi_{\alpha\beta}(t)
does not necessarily satisfy the adiabaticity conditions, we generically write

\dot{c}_{\alpha}(t)=a_{\beta}(t)c_{\beta}(t)+\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\neq\beta}a_{\alpha^{\prime}}(t)c_{\alpha^{\prime}}(t)
, with
a_{\alpha^{\prime}}(t)
and
a_{\beta}(t)

complex coefficients. However, by imposing
c_{\beta}(0)=0
,
it yields
{c}_{\beta}(t)=0
and consequently

\dot{c}_{\alpha}(t)=\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\neq\beta}a_{\alpha^{\prime}}(t)c_{\alpha^{\prime}}(t)
. By iteratively applying this argument after decoupling
\lambda_{\beta}(t)
,
we finally obtain that
\dot{c}_{\eta}(t)=a_{\eta}(t)c_{\eta}(t)
, stopping as
\lambda_{\eta}(t)
becomes the largest eigenvalue of the remaining set.
Thus, by assuming a single
c_{\eta}(0)\neq 0
in the initial state, which is equivalent to assuming an initial superposition of only two Jordan blocks, an adiabatic dynamics
for
t\rightarrow\infty
is always achieved, reading

|\rho(t)\rangle\rangle=|\pazocal{D}_{0}\rangle\rangle+c_{\eta}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\eta}(t)\rangle\rangle\text{ . }

(174)

10
Thermodynamics of adiabatic dynamics in open system

Thermodynamics is the knowledge field that encompasses physical processes of systems composed by a large number of particles subject to interaction with its environment. In particular, processes where some amount of energy exchanges that may arise from the difference of temperature between two systems (heat) or due to changes in force fields acting on some system (work). In this sense, a new knowledge field emerges when we use notions of thermodynamics to quantum mechanical systems:
quantum thermodynamics
. Quantum thermodynamics deals with thermodynamics quantities (heat and work) of systems that present physical properties inherent to quantum systems (entanglement and coherence, for example). For example, quantum thermodynamics may be used to understand how coherence allows us to design efficient thermal engines at quantum level
[
126
,
127
]
, and entanglement is used to enhance charging power in quantum batteries
[
89
,
90
,
88
]
. Here we will consider a particular question related with the thermodynamics of adiabatic dynamics. To this end, we present a short discussion on how to define heat and work for quantum mechanical systems driven by master equations as in Eq. (
19
). Because it is not the scope of this thesis to carefully present general results and features of quantum thermodynamics, for further details on quantum thermodynamic and its applications we recommend some relevant references
[
128
,
129
,
130
,
131
,
132
]
.

10.1
Work and heat in quantum thermodynamics

In classical thermodynamics, the first thermodynamics law establishes that the internal energy variation of a system is given by

dU=d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptW+d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ\text{ , }

(175)

where
d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ
is some amount of heat exchange between the system of interest and its environment, and
d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptW
is work realized by/on the system. Here
d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004pt
denotes that the heat and word depend on the thermodynamics path followed by the system. In above equation, heat and work are very well defined and it can be obtained from observation macroscopic properties of the system. However, physical quantities in quantum mechanics are obtained from its postulates and, consequently, from Hermitean observables. In general, it has been shown that it is not possible to associate an observable for the thermodynamic definition of heat and of work
[
133
]
. Then, the starting point widely used to define such physical quantities in quantum systems is from the definition of internal energy
[
134
,
135
]
. If we consider a quantum system driven by a time-dependent Hamiltonian
H(t)
, we can can compute the instantaneous internal energy of the system as

U(t)=\text{Tr}\left[\rho(t)H(t)\right]\text{ , }

(176)

given the instantaneous system state at instant
t
. Therefore, the energy variation of the system can be directly obtained as

dU(t)=\frac{d}{dt}\text{Tr}\left[\rho(t)H(t)\right]dt=\text{Tr}\left[\dot{\rho}(t)H(t)\right]dt+\text{Tr}\left[\rho(t)\dot{H}(t)\right]dt\text{ . }

(177)

Now, in order to get a relation between the above equation with Eq. (
175
), we need to consider two important observations. First, let be the system evolving free from any external interaction with other systems, that is, we have a closed system and it follows a unitary dynamics. In this case, we get

dU_{\text{cs}}(t)=\text{Tr}\left[\rho(t)\dot{H}(t)\right]dt\text{ , }

(178)

because we can use the von Neumann equation for the density operator to write

\text{Tr}\left[\dot{\rho}(t)H(t)\right]=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\text{Tr}\left[[H(t),\rho(t)]H(t)\right]=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\text{Tr}\left[H(t)\rho(t)H(t)-\rho(t)H(t)H(t)\right]=0\text{ , }

(179)

valid whenever the system dynamics is unitary (closed system). That means any variation in the internal energy of the system, through a unitary dynamics, is due to time-dependence of the Hamiltonian. Consequently, some external field control is required when we want to change the system energy. For this reason, it is convenient to associate the energy variation
\Delta U_{\text{cs}}(t_{1},t_{0})
in interval
t\in[t_{1},t_{0}]
with work realized by/on the system
W(t_{1},t_{0})
as

W(t_{1},t_{0})=\Delta U_{\text{cs}}(t_{1},t_{0})=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\underbrace{\text{Tr}\left[\rho(t)\dot{H}(t)\right]dt}_{d\hskip-1.31114pt\bar{}\hskip 1.31114ptW}\text{ . }

(180)

Because we have an addition in Eq. (
175
), when
W(t_{1},t_{0})>0
we say that work is done
on
the system, otherwise work is done
by
the system. Here we need to be careful because the above equation is valid for unitary dynamics, so when the system is coupled to an external system, we cannot identity the above equation as work in general
[
128
,
131
]
. On the other hand, let us consider the case where the system is driven with a
time-independent
Hamiltonian
H(t)=H
, then from Eq. (
177
) we get

dU_{\text{os}}(t)=\text{Tr}\left[\dot{\rho}(t)H\right]dt=\text{Tr}\left[\pazocal{L}_{t}[\rho(t)]H\right]dt\text{ , }

(181)

where we used the Eq. (
19
) in last equality. The meaning of the subscript “os” in above equation is because
dU_{\text{os}}(t)\neq 0
if the system is coupled to some external system (reservoir), otherwise we get
dU_{\text{os}}(t)=0
. In fact, this result is immediate from Eq. (
178
) with
H(t)=H
. Then, when the system is driven by time-independent fields, the energy of our system just change if we couple it to some external system which will transfer energy to our system of interest. For this reason, we identify the above quantity with the amount of
heat
exchanged between the system and its environment as

Q(t_{1},t_{0})=\Delta U_{\text{os}}(t_{1},t_{0})=\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\underbrace{\text{Tr}\left[\pazocal{L}_{t}[\rho(t)]H\right]dt}_{d\hskip-1.31114pt\bar{}\hskip 1.31114ptQ}\text{ . }

(182)

An important remark here is that in
steady state
regime we get
d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ=0
. In fact, because we have that the steady state
\rho_{\text{ss}}
satisfies
\pazocal{L}_{t}[\rho_{\text{ss}}]=0
, in the limit where
\pazocal{L}_{t}[\rho(t)]\rightarrow\pazocal{L}_{t}[\rho_{\text{ss}}]=0
, we get
d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ=0
. From this result, if the dynamics admits a steady state, we can establish a maximum amount of heat exchanged between system and reservoir as

Q_{\text{ss}}=\int_{t_{0}}^{\tau_{\text{ss}}}\text{Tr}\left[\pazocal{L}_{t}[\rho(t)]H\right]dt=\text{Tr}\left[H\rho_{\text{ss}}\right]-\text{Tr}\left[H\rho(t_{0})\right]\text{ . }

(183)

where
\tau_{\text{ss}}
is the time required to achieve the steady state
\rho_{\text{ss}}
.

10.2
Adiabatic process
versus
adiabatic dynamics

In thermodynamics, by definition adiabaticity is associated to a process with no heat exchange between the system and its reservoir. On the other hand, as discussed in Sec.
8
, adiabaticity in dynamics of quantum systems is well defined in a different way. Therefore, the question that arises here is:
How are thermodynamics adiabatic processes related with an adiabatic dynamics?
As a preliminary result (but insufficient yet), we can restrict our analysis to the closed system case. In general, as already discussed here, unitary processes do not admit energy transference as heat, since no system is coupled to our main system. Because this result is independent on the kind of dynamics (adiabatic or not), we can say that
adiabatic processes in closed systems are not related with an adiabatic dynamics
. Therefore, in order to give a more complete study on this issue, it is convenient to consider an open system dynamics because some heat exchange is admissible. To this end, we need to introduce heat and work in a general way from superoperator formalism, where adiabatic dynamics in open system is well defined.

Therefore, let us consider the heat exchange as given in Eq. (
181
). To derive the corresponding expression in the superoperator formalism we first define the basis of operators given by
\{\sigma_{i}\}
,
i=0,\cdots,D_{-}
(again we are using
D_{-}=D^{2}-1
), where
\text{Tr}[\sigma^{\dagger}_{i}\sigma_{j}]=D\,\delta_{ij}
. In this basis, we can write
\rho(t)
and
H(t)
generically as

H(t)=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{n=0}^{D_{-}}h_{n}(t)\sigma^{\dagger}_{n}\text{ \, and \, }\rho(t)=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{n=0}^{D_{-}}\varrho_{n}(t)\sigma_{n}\text{ , }

(184)

where we have
h_{n}(t)=\text{Tr}[H(t)\sigma_{n}]
and
\varrho_{n}(t)=\text{Tr}[\rho(t)\sigma^{\dagger}_{n}]
. Then, we get

d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ=\frac{1}{D^{2}}\left(\sum_{n,m=0}^{D_{-}}\text{Tr}[\pazocal{L}[\varrho_{n}(t)\sigma_{n}]h_{m}(t)\sigma^{\dagger}_{m}]\right)dt=\frac{1}{D^{2}}\left(\sum_{n,m=0}^{D_{-}}\varrho_{n}(t)h_{m}(t)\text{Tr}[\pazocal{L}[\sigma_{n}]\sigma^{\dagger}_{m}]\right)dt\text{ .}

(185)

Now, we use the definition of the matrix elements of the superoperator
\mathbbm{L}(t)
, associated with
\pazocal{L}[\bullet]
, which reads
\mathbbm{L}_{mn}=(1/D)\text{Tr}[\sigma^{\dagger}_{m}\pazocal{L}[\sigma_{n}]]
, so that we write

d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ=\frac{1}{D}\left(\sum_{n,m=0}^{D_{-}}h_{m}(t)\mathbbm{L}_{mn}\varrho_{n}(t)\right)dt\text{ .}

(186)

In conclusion, by defining the vector elements

\displaystyle\langle\langle h(t)|

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}h_{0}(t)&h_{1}(t)&\cdots&h_{D_{-}}(t)\end{bmatrix}^{\text{t}}\text{ , }

(187)

\displaystyle|\rho(t)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\varrho_{0}(t)&\varrho_{1}(t)&\cdots&\varrho_{D_{-}}(t)\end{bmatrix}\text{ , }

(188)

we can rewrite Eq. (
186
), yielding

d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ=\frac{1}{D}\langle\langle h(t)|\mathbbm{L}(t)|\rho(t)\rangle\rangle dt\text{ . }

(189)

Equivalently, from Eq. (
180
) we can use the Eq. (
184
) to write
\dot{H}(t)=(1/D)\sum_{n=0}^{D_{-}}\dot{h}_{n}(t)\sigma^{\dagger}_{n}
and, consequently

d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptW=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{n=0}^{D_{-}}\dot{h}_{n}(t)\text{Tr}[\rho(t)\sigma^{\dagger}_{n}]dt=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{n=0}^{D_{-}}\dot{h}_{n}(t)\varrho_{n}(t)dt\text{ , }

(190)

where we used the definition of the coefficients
\varrho_{n}(t)
in last equality. Now, by using Eqs. (
187
) and (
188
) into above equation, we conclude that

d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptW=\frac{1}{D}\langle\langle\dot{h}(t)|\rho(t)\rangle\rangle dt\text{ . }

(191)

To end, since the system is evolving under a non-unitary dynamics, some entropy variation is computed along the evolution. Here we use the entropy variation as provided by the variation in Von Neumann entropy given by

\Delta S(t,t_{0})=S(t)-S(t_{0})=-\text{Tr}\left[\rho(t)\ln[\rho(t)]\right]+\text{Tr}\left[\rho(t_{0})\ln[\rho(t_{0})]\right]\text{ , }

(192)

that can be written in a differential way as

dS(t)=-\left[\frac{d}{dt}\text{Tr}\left[\rho(t)\ln[\rho(t)]\right]\right]dt=-\text{Tr}[\dot{\rho}(t)\log\rho(t)]-\text{Tr}[\dot{\rho}(t)]\text{ , }

(193)

and by using that
\text{Tr}[\dot{\rho}(t)]=0
, because
\text{Tr}[\rho(t)]=1
, and
\dot{\rho}(t)=\pazocal{L}_{t}[\rho(t)]
we get

dS(t)=-\text{Tr}[\dot{\rho}(t)\log\rho(t)]dt=-\text{Tr}[\pazocal{L}_{t}[\rho(t)]\log\rho(t)]dt\text{ . }

(194)

Now, let us to write

\log\rho(t)=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{n=0}^{D_{-}}\varrho_{n}^{\log}(t)\sigma_{n}^{\dagger}\text{ , }

(195)

so that we can define the vectors
\langle\langle\rho_{\log}(t)|
associated to
\log\rho(t)
with components
\varrho_{n}^{\log}(t)
obtained as
\varrho_{n}^{\log}(t)=\text{Tr}[\sigma_{n}\log\rho(t)]
. Thus, we get

dS(t)=-\frac{1}{D^{2}}\sum_{m=0}^{D_{-}}\sum_{n=0}^{D_{-}}\varrho_{m}(t)\varrho_{n}^{\log}(t)\text{Tr}[\pazocal{L}_{t}[\sigma_{m}]\sigma_{n}^{\dagger}]dt=-\frac{1}{D}\langle\langle\rho_{\log}(t)|\mathbbm{L}(t)|\rho(t)\rangle\rangle dt\text{ . }

(196)

Thus, from Eqs. (
185
), (
191
) and (
196
) we can study the thermodynamics of adiabatic dynamics in a general framework. However, since work of an adiabatic dynamics has been addressed in a number of applications, here we are interested in study the implications of the adiabatic dynamics in the expression of heat derived in Eqs. (
185
) and (
196
). To this end, let us consider the most general case in which the system starts from a superposition
|\rho(0)\rangle\rangle=\sum\nolimits_{\alpha,k_{\alpha}}r^{k_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}|\pazocal{D}^{k_{\alpha}}_{\alpha}(0)\rangle\rangle
, associated with the initial arbitrary matrix density
\rho(0)
. Then, if we take into account the situations where the system evolves adiabatically, we use the evolution operator in Eqs. (
135
) and (
134
) to write the evolved as

\displaystyle|\rho_{\text{ad}}(t)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\pazocal{U}(t)|\rho(0)\rangle\rangle=\sum_{\beta}^{N}\sum_{k_{\beta}}^{N_{\beta}}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\sum_{n_{\alpha},m_{\alpha}}^{N_{\alpha}}r^{k_{\beta}}_{\beta}\eta_{n_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{n_{\alpha}}(t)\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{m_{\alpha}}(0)|\pazocal{D}^{k_{\beta}}_{\beta}(0)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}^{N}\sum_{n_{\beta},k_{\beta}}^{N_{\beta}}r^{k_{\beta}}_{\beta}\eta_{n_{\beta}k_{\beta}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}^{n_{\beta}}(t)\rangle\rangle

(197)

where we defined
\eta_{n_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}}(t)=e^{\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{\alpha}(\xi)d\xi}u_{n_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}}(t)
. By using the above result, from Eq. (
189
) we write

d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ_{\text{ad}}=\frac{1}{D}\langle\langle h(t)|\mathbbm{L}(t)|\rho_{\text{ad}}(t)\rangle\rangle dt=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{\beta}^{N}\sum_{n_{\beta},k_{\beta}}^{N_{\beta}}r^{k_{\beta}}_{\beta}\eta_{n_{\beta}k_{\beta}}(t)\langle\langle h(t)|\mathbbm{L}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}^{n_{\beta}}(t)\rangle\rangle dt\text{ , }

(198)

as the amount of heat exchanged between the system and its environment along an adiabatic dynamics in open systems. A more useful expression can be found using the quasi-eigenvector equation for
\mathbbm{L}(t)
given in Eq. (
30
) and reads as

d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ_{\text{ad}}(t)=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{\beta}^{N}\sum_{n_{\beta},k_{\beta}}^{N_{\beta}}r^{k_{\beta}}_{\beta}\eta_{n_{\beta}k_{\beta}}(t)\left[\langle\langle h(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}^{n_{\beta}-1}(t)\rangle\rangle+\lambda_{\beta}(t)\langle\langle h(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}^{n_{\beta}}(t)\rangle\rangle\right]dt\text{ . }

(199)

Moreover, let us consider the case where we have an one-dimensional Jordan block decomposition of
\mathbbm{L}(t)
. In this case, we use the adiabatic solution in Eq. (
189
) to write

d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ_{\text{ad}}^{\text{1D}}(t)=\frac{1}{D}\langle\langle h(t)|\mathbbm{L}(t)|\rho^{1\text{D}}_{\text{ad}}(t)\rangle\rangle dt=\frac{1}{D}\sum_{\beta}^{N}r_{\beta}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\Lambda_{\beta}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}\lambda_{\beta}(t)\langle\langle h(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}^{n_{\beta}}(t)\rangle\rangle dt\text{ . }

(200)

The Eqs. (
199
) and (
200
) constitute the main result of this section, where it is evident the possibility of heat exchange during an adiabatic dynamics in open system. In addition, the Eq. (
200
) allows us to find a condition for achieving an adiabatic process through an adiabatic dynamics. In fact, note that if we start the dynamics from a superposition of eigenvectors
\{\pazocal{D}_{\beta}^{n_{\beta}}(0)\}
of
\mathbbm{L}(0)
with
\lambda_{\beta}(t)=0
for all
t
, we get
d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ_{\text{ad}}^{\text{1D}}(t)=0
for all
t
. In conclusion, we can establish that
an adiabatic dynamics in quantum mechanics is not in general associated with an adiabatic process in quantum thermodynamics, with a sufficient condition for thermal adiabaticity being the evolution within an eigenstate set with vanishing eigenvalue of
\mathbbm{L}(t)
.

To end, from Eq. (
196
), we can write the von Neumann entropy variation as

dS(t)=-\frac{1}{D}\langle\langle\rho^{\text{ad}}_{\log}(t)|\mathbbm{L}(t)|\rho^{\text{ad}}(t)\rangle\rangle dt=-\frac{1}{D}\sum\nolimits_{i,k_{i}}c^{(k_{i})}_{i}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\lambda}_{i,k_{i}}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}\langle\langle\rho^{\text{ad}}_{\log}(t)|\mathbbm{L}(t)|\pazocal{D}^{(k_{i})}_{i}(t)\rangle\rangle dt\text{ , }

so that we can use the Eq. (
30
) to write

dS(t)=\frac{1}{D}\sum\nolimits_{i,k_{i}}c^{(k_{i})}_{i}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\lambda}_{i,k_{i}}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}\Gamma_{i,k_{i}}(t)dt\text{ , }

(201)

where
\Gamma_{i,k_{i}}(t)=\langle\langle\rho^{\text{ad}}_{\log}(t)|\pazocal{D}^{(k_{i}-1)}_{i}(t)\rangle\rangle+\lambda_{i}(t)\langle\langle\rho^{\text{ad}}_{\log}(t)|\pazocal{D}^{(k_{i})}_{i}(t)\rangle\rangle
, with
\langle\langle\rho^{\text{ad}}_{\log}(t)|
standing for the adiabatic evolved state associated with
\langle\langle\rho_{\log}(t)|
.

Thermal adiabaticity for a qubit adiabatic dynamics –
As an illustration of how adiabatic dynamics is an adiabatic process, let us begin by considering a two-level system initialized in a thermal equilibrium
state
\rho_{\text{th}}(0)
for the Hamiltonian
H(0)
at inverse temperature
\beta\!=\!1/k_{\text{B}}T
, where
k_{\text{B}}
and
T
are the
Boltzmann’s constant and the absolute temperature, respectively. Now, let the system be governed by a Lindblad
equation, where the environment acts as a
dephasing
channel in the energy eigenstate basis

\{|E_{n}(t)\rangle\}
of
H(t)
. Thus, we describe the coupling between the system and its reservoir through

\dot{\rho}(t)=\pazocal{L}[\rho(t)]=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[H(t),\rho(t)]+\gamma(t)\left[\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)\rho(t)\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)-\rho(t)\right]\text{ , }

(202)

where
\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)=|E_{1}(t)\rangle\langle E_{1}(t)|-|E_{0}(t)\rangle\langle E_{0}(t)|
, with
|E_{n}(t)\rangle
being the instantaneous eigenstates of
H(t)
, here we are assuming
E_{1}(t)>E_{0}(t)
for all
t
. In this case, the set of eigenvectors of
\pazocal{L}[\bullet]

can be obtained from set of operators
P_{nm}(t)=|E_{n}(t)\rangle\langle E_{m}(t)|
, because we have

\displaystyle\pazocal{L}[P_{nm}(t)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[H(t),P_{nm}(t)]+\gamma(t)\left[\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)P_{nm}(t)\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)-P_{nm}(t)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}\left[E_{n}(t)-E_{m}(t)\right]P_{nm}(t)+\gamma(t)\left[\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)P_{nm}(t)\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)-P_{nm}(t)\right]P_{nm}(t)

(203)

Now, by computing the second term in above equation we get

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)P_{nm}(t)\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)

\displaystyle=\left[|E_{1}(t)\rangle\langle E_{1}(t)|-|E_{0}(t)\rangle\langle E_{0}(t)|\right]|E_{n}(t)\rangle\langle E_{m}(t)|\left[|E_{1}(t)\rangle\langle E_{1}(t)|-|E_{0}(t)\rangle\langle E_{0}(t)|\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\delta_{1n}|E_{1}(t)\rangle-\delta_{0n}|E_{0}(t)\rangle\right]\left[\delta_{1m}\langle E_{1}(t)|-\delta_{0m}\langle E_{0}(t)|\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0,1}\delta_{kn}\delta_{km}|E_{k}(t)\rangle\langle E_{k}(t)|-\sum_{k=0,1}\delta_{kn}\delta_{\bar{k}m}|E_{k}(t)\rangle\langle E_{\bar{k}}(t)|\text{ , }

(204)

so that we can write

\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)P_{nm}(t)\Gamma^{\text{dp}}(t)=\left\{\begin{matrix}P_{nn}(t)&\text{for }n=m\\
-P_{nm}(t)&\text{for }n\neq m\end{matrix}\right.\text{ , }

(205)

where we conclude that
P_{nm}(t)
satisfies the eigenvalue equation
\pazocal{L}[P_{nm}(t)]=\lambda_{nm}(t)P_{nm}(t)
with

\lambda_{nm}(t)=\left\{\begin{matrix}0&\text{for }n=m\\
\left[E_{n}(t)-E_{m}(t)\right]/i\hbar-2\gamma(t)&\text{for }n\neq m\end{matrix}\right.\text{ . }

(206)

Therefore, by writing
P_{nm}(t)
in superoperator formalism the of
|\pazocal{D}_{nm}(t)\rangle\rangle
, with components
\pazocal{D}^{(i)}_{nm}(t)=\text{Tr}\left[P_{nm}(t)\sigma_{i}\right]
, the eigenvalue equation for
\mathbbm{L}(t)
can be written as

\mathbbm{L}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{nm}(t)\rangle\rangle=\lambda_{nm}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{nm}(t)\rangle\rangle\text{ , }

(207)

where
\lambda_{nm}(t)=E_{n}(t)-E_{m}(t)-2(1-\delta_{nm})\gamma(t)
. Since we are considering the initial state as a thermal state, in the superoperator formalism the initial state
\rho_{\text{th}}(0)
, we can write

\rho_{\text{th}}(0)=\pazocal{Z}^{-1}(0)\sum_{n}e^{-\beta E_{n}(0)}P_{nn}(0)\text{ \, \, }\Rightarrow\text{ \, \, }|\rho_{\text{th}}(0)\rangle\rangle=\pazocal{Z}^{-1}(0)\sum_{n}e^{-\beta E_{n}(0)}|\pazocal{D}_{nn}(0)\rangle\rangle\text{ , }

(208)

where
\mathcal{Z}(0)=\text{Tr}\left[e^{-\beta H(0)}\right]
is the
partition function of the system. Note that, since
|\rho_{\text{th}}(0)\rangle\rangle
is given by a superposition of eigenvectors of
\mathbbm{L}(t)
with eigenvalue
\lambda_{nn}(t)=0
for all
t
, we obtain from Eq. (
200
) that
d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ_{\text{ad}}=0
for all
t
whenever the evolution is adiabatic. Therefore,
thermal adiabaticity is achieved for an arbitrary open-system adiabatic dynamics subject to dephasing in the energy eigenbasis.
Hence, any internal energy variation for this situation should be identified as
work
.

10.3
Experimental realization of heat exchange in adiabatic dynamics

Now, we will consider a case where the open system dynamics is adiabatic and some amount of heat is exchanged between system and reservoir. The system used here is the same as before, a two level system evolving under dephasing. However, here we conveniently change the decohering basis and now the decohering acts in computational basis
|0\rangle
and
|1\rangle
, instead the eigenstate basis of
H(t)
. Then the dynamics of the system is given by

\dot{\rho}(t)=\pazocal{L}[\rho(t)]=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[H_{x},\rho(t)]+\gamma(t)\left[\sigma_{z}\rho(t)\sigma_{z}-\rho(t)\right]\text{ , }

(209)

with
H_{x}=\hbar\omega\sigma_{x}
. Since we are interested in studying the heat transfer during the adiabatic dynamics, we drive the system with a time-independent Hamiltonian, so that
d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptW=0
for all
t
and therefore any variation in internal energy of the system is due to heat flux. Moreover, because we want to drive the system through an adiabatic dynamics, the parameter
\gamma(t)
needs to be time-dependent.

The system is initialized in the thermal of
H_{\text{x}}
at inverse temperature
\beta
written as

\rho(0)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbbm{1}+\tanh[\beta\hbar\omega]\sigma_{x}\right)\text{ , }

(210)

where rewrite the above state in superoperator formalism as the state
|\rho(0)\rangle\rangle
in the basis
\mathcal{O}_{\text{tls}}
as

|\rho(0)\rangle\rangle=|\sigma_{0}\rangle\rangle-\tanh[\beta\hbar\omega]|\sigma_{x}\rangle\rangle\text{ , }

(211)

with the basis
|\sigma_{k}\rangle\rangle
(
k=\{0,x,y,z\}
) defined in Eq. (
23
). In this dynamics, the superoperator
\mathbbm{L}(t)
associated with the generator
\pazocal{L}[\bullet]
reads

\mathbbm{L}(t)=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0&0\\
0&-2\gamma(t)&0&0\\
0&0&-2\gamma(t)&-2\omega\\
0&0&2\omega&0\end{bmatrix}\text{ . }

(212)

It is possible to show that the set
\{|\sigma_{0}\rangle\rangle,|\sigma_{x}\rangle\rangle\}
satisfies the eigenvalue equation for
\mathbbm{L}(t)
as

\mathbbm{L}(t)|\sigma_{0}\rangle\rangle=0\text{ \ \ , \ \ }\mathbbm{L}(t)|\sigma_{x}\rangle\rangle=-2\gamma(t)|\sigma_{x}\rangle\rangle\text{ , }

(213)

so, if the dynamics is adiabatic, we can write the evolved state as
|\rho_{\text{ad}}(t)\rangle\rangle=r_{1}(t)|\sigma_{0}\rangle\rangle+r_{x}(t)|\sigma_{x}\rangle\rangle
, where
r_{y}(t)=r_{y}(0)=0
and
r_{z}(t)=r_{z}(0)=0
because the coefficients evolve independently form each other. Thus, from the adiabatic solution in open quantum system given in Eq. (
114
),
we obtain
r_{1}(t)=1
and
r_{x}(0)=-\tanh[\beta\hbar\omega]
, so that we can use
\tilde{\lambda}_{1}=0
and
\tilde{\lambda}_{x}=-2\gamma(t)
to obtain

|\rho_{\text{ad}}(t)\rangle\rangle=|\sigma_{0}\rangle\rangle-e^{-2\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(\xi)d\xi}\tanh[\beta\hbar\omega]|\sigma_{x}\rangle\rangle\text{ . }

(214)

Notice that by rewriting the above equation in the standard operator formalism, one gets

\rho_{\text{ad}}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\mathbbm{1}-e^{-2\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(\xi)d\xi}\tanh(\beta\hbar\omega)\sigma_{x}\right]\text{ , }

(215)

moreover, by using this formalism, it is also possible to show that the dephasing channel can be used as a thermalization process if we suitably choose the parameter
\gamma(t)
and the total evolution time
\tau_{\text{dec}}
. In fact, we can define a new inverse temperature
\beta_{\text{deph}}
so that Eq. (
214
) behaves as thermal state, namely,

|\rho(t)\rangle\rangle=|\sigma_{0}\rangle\rangle-\tanh[\beta_{\text{deph}}\hbar\omega]|\sigma_{x}\rangle\rangle\text{ , }

(216)

where we immediately identify

\beta_{\text{deph}}=\frac{1}{\hbar\omega}\text{arctanh}\left[e^{-2\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(\xi)d\xi}\tanh(\beta\hbar\omega)\right]\text{ . }

(217)

Now, we use the Eq. (
199
) to compute the exchanged heat during an infinitesimal time interval
dt
as

d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ_{\text{ad}}(t)=2\hbar\tanh(\beta\hbar\omega)\omega\gamma(t)e^{-2\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(\xi)d\xi}dt\text{ , }

(218)

and therefore we get the total exchanged heat as a function of the total evolution time
\tau_{\text{dec}}
in which the system remains coupled to reservoir as

Q_{\text{ad}}(\tau_{\text{dec}})=\int_{0}^{\tau_{\text{dec}}}\left(\frac{d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ_{\text{ad}}(t)}{dt}\right)dt=\hbar\omega\tanh(\beta\hbar\omega)\left(1-e^{-2\bar{\gamma}\tau_{\text{dec}}}\right)\text{ , }

(219)

where
\bar{\gamma}=(1/\tau_{\text{dec}})\int_{0}^{\tau_{\text{dec}}}\gamma(\xi)d\xi
is the average dephasing rate during
\tau_{\text{dec}}
. A first remark here is that
Q_{\text{ad}}(\tau_{\text{dec}})>0
for any value of
\bar{\gamma}>0
and
\tau
, so this means that the reservoir here works as an
artificial
thermal reservoir at inverse temperature
\tilde{\beta}=\beta_{\text{deph}}<\beta
. We also considered a similar analysis in an experimental simulation of a thermal engine in all optical experimental setup
[
136
]
. In addition, due to the negative argument in the exponential, the higher the mean-value of
\gamma(t)
the faster the heat exchange ends. Then, we can further compute the maximum exchanged heat from Eq. (
219
), in the limit
\bar{\gamma}\tau_{\text{dec}}\gg 1
, as a quantity independent of the environment parameters and given by
Q_{\text{max}}=\hbar\omega\tanh(\beta\hbar\omega)
. This result can be also obtained from Eqs. (
183
) and (
215
) by using the asymptotic steady state
\rho_{\text{ss}}=\rho_{\text{ad}}(t\rightarrow\infty)\propto\mathbbm{1}
. It would be worth highlighting that, for quantum thermal machines weakly coupled to thermal reservoirs at different temperatures
[
134
]
, the maximum heat
Q_{\text{max}}
is obtained with high-temperature hot reservoirs
[
137
,
138
,
139
]
.

The experimental realization of the above results was done through the trapped ion system already used previously, but in this case we need to consider an additional challenger due to the parameter
\gamma(t)
to be time-dependent. To do that, it is used the same procedure as before with an additional element able to provide a time-dependent decohering quantum channel with high controllability. For further details, please see Appendix
7.A.4
. In Fig.
12
we show the experimental results for the heat exchange
Q_{\text{ad}}(\tau_{\text{dec}})
as a function of
\tau_{\text{dec}}
, where we have chosen
\gamma(t)\!=\!\gamma_{0}(1+t/\tau_{\text{dec}})
, where here the parameter
\tau_{\text{dec}}
is experimentally controlled through the time interval associated to the action of our decohering quantum channel. The solid curves in Fig.
12
are computed from Eq. (
219
), while the experimental points are directly computed through the variation of internal energy as
Q(\tau_{\text{dec}})\!=\!U_{\text{fin}}-U_{\text{ini}}
, where
U_{\text{fin(ini)}}\!=\!\text{Tr}[\rho_{\text{fin(ini)}}H_{x}]
. The computation of
U_{\text{fin(ini)}}
is directly obtained from quantum state tomography of
\rho_{\text{fin(ini)}}
for each value of
\tau_{\text{dec}}
. Although the maximum exchanged heat is independent of
\gamma_{0}
, the initial dephasing rate
\gamma_{0}
affects the
power
for which the system exchanges heat with the reservoir for a given evolution time
\tau_{\text{dec}}
.

Figure 12:
Heat
Q_{\text{ad}}(\tau_{\text{dec}})
as a function of the total evolution time
\tau_{\text{dec}}
for several values of the parameter
\gamma_{0}
. We use
\hbar\omega=82.662
peV and
\beta^{-1}=17.238
peV, with the physical constants
\hbar\approx 6.578\cdot 10^{-16}
eV
\cdot
s and
k_{\text{B}}\approx 8.619\cdot 10^{-5}
eV/K.

By completeness and to provide a more detailed view of this heat exchange, we analyze the von Neumann entropy during the evolution. From Eq. (
196
), for the particular case of an one-dimensional Jordan block decomposition, we write the entropy variation in our dynamics as

dS(t)=\frac{1}{2}\sum\nolimits_{i={0}}^{1}c_{i}e^{\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{\lambda}_{i}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}}\lambda_{i}(t)\langle\langle\rho^{\text{ad}}_{\log}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{i}(t)\rangle\rangle dt\text{ , }

(220)

where we used that
\Gamma_{i}(t)=\lambda_{i}(t)\langle\langle\rho^{\text{ad}}_{\log}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{i}(t)\rangle\rangle
. By computing
\langle\langle\rho^{\text{Ad}}_{\log}(t)|
, where we find

\langle\langle\rho^{\text{Ad}}_{\log}(t)|=\log\left(\frac{1-g^{2}(t)}{4}\right)\langle\langle 1|-2\text{arctanh}[g(t)]\langle\langle x|\text{ , }

(221)

with
g(t)\!=\!e^{-2\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(\xi)d\xi}\tanh(\beta\hbar\omega)
. Then, from the set of adopted values for our parameters and the spectrum of the Lindbladian, we conclude that

dS(t)=4g(t)\gamma(t)\text{arctanh}[g(t)]dt\text{ . }

(222)

Notice that the relation between heat and entropy can be obtained by rewriting the exchanged heat
dQ
in the interval
dt
as

d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ_{\text{ad}}(t)=2\hbar\omega\gamma(t)g(t)dt
. In conclusion, the energy variation can indeed be identified as heat exchanged along the adiabatic dynamics. Indeed, by computing the thermodynamic relation between
dS(t)
and
d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ_{\text{ad}}(t)
we get
dS(t)=\beta_{\text{deph}}d\hskip-1.60004pt\bar{}\hskip 1.60004ptQ_{\text{ad}}(t)
, where
\beta_{\text{deph}}
is the inverse temperature of the simulated thermal bath shown in Eq. (
217
).

To guarantee that the amount of heat shown in Fig.
12
are associated with an exchange heat in an adiabatic dynamics, we computed the fidelity
\pazocal{F}(\tau_{\text{dec}})
between the evolved state (solution of the Eq. (
209
)) and the adiabatic solution given in Eq. (
215
). In Table
1
we show the minimum value of the fidelity along the evolution, that is, we compute the quantity
\pazocal{F}_{\text{min}}=\min_{\tau_{\text{dec}}}\pazocal{F}(\tau_{\text{dec}})
. From Table
1
it is possible to see that all of heat exchange behavior shown in Fig.
12
are associated with an adiabatic dynamics in open system with high fidelity.

Minimum value of the experimental fidelity
\pazocal{F}_{\text{min}}=\min_{\tau_{\text{dec}}}\pazocal{F}(\tau_{\text{dec}})
for each choice of
\gamma_{0}
. The maximum experimental error
\Delta\pazocal{F}_{\text{min}}
for
\pazocal{F}_{\text{min}}
is about
\Delta\pazocal{F}_{\text{min}}=0.13\%
of
\pazocal{F}_{\text{min}}
.
