. By using the generalized TQD Hamiltonian
[
71
]
, we have presented the optimal solution in terms of pulse sequence and resources to implement fast quantum gates through counter-diabatic quantum computation with high fidelity. The energy-optimal protocol presented here is potentially useful for speeding up digitized adiabatic quantum computing
[
73
]
. Our study explicitly illustrates the performance of generalized TQD in terms of both energy resources and optimal pulse sequence. Since digitized adiabatic quantum computing requires the Trotterization of the adiabatic dynamics, our protocol could be useful in reducing the number of steps used in digital adiabatic quantum processes. In addition, such process is independent of the experimental approach used to digitize the adiabatic quantum evolution.

These results are encouraging for the generalized transitionless approach in the open-system realm as long as local Hamiltonians
are possible to be designed. In the specific case of quantum gate Hamiltonians, this approach can be applied, e.g. to derive robust local building blocks for analog implementations of quantum circuits (see, e.g., Refs.
[
157
,
8
]
).

Chapter 5
Overview and future perspectives

I
n this thesis we presented general results on adiabatic dynamics in closed and open systems, and shortcuts to adiabaticity by transitionless quantum driving. In the context of adiabatic dynamics in closed system we presented potentially useful theorems that can be viewed as a validation mechanism for the well-known adiabaticity conditions, where changes of frame in Schrödinger equation are required. In particular, for the closed system case, we have both theoretically and experimentally shown that
several relevant ACs, which include the traditional AC, are neither sufficient nor necessary to
describe the adiabatic behavior of a qubit in an oscillating field considered here. This result leads us to the conclusion that we do not typically have a fully applicable AC. However, from an adequate choice of the reference frame used to describe the system dynamics, sufficiency and
necessity of the ACs are fundamentally obtained under certain conditions through the non-inertial frame map. As a potential application of adiabatic dynamics, we provided a way to enhance the charging performance of quantum devices able to store energy (quantum batteries). We propose the notion of
adiabatic QBs
, where the adiabatic behavior takes place in the non-inertial frame and it allows for a stable charging process of the battery, since in inertial frame the dynamics is not adiabatic. To this aim, we propose the notion of a new quantum observable that quantifies the energy transfer rate from the quantum battery to a consumption hub, the power operator. Applications of this operator have demonstrated its usefulness in quantum battery context. For open systems, our theoretical results give complementary contributions to the results known in the literature. The validity conditions used to predict the adiabatic behavior of a quantum system evolving through non-unitary dynamics are reviewed. We have shown that the adiabatic approximation for open systems holds in the asymptotic time limit
t\rightarrow\infty
as an arbitrarily valid consequence of the one-dimensional Jordan decomposition of the Lindblad superoperator, the absence of level crossings, and the initialization of the system as a superposition of only two eigenstates of Lindbladian that drives the system. Our main result in adiabaticity in open system refers to the application in quantum thermodynamics, where we can provide some enhanced understanding on the thermal adiabatic process and dynamical adiabatic evolutions. To end, as an original contribution to the TQD theory, we have developed a generalized minimal energy demanding counter-diabatic theory, which is able to yield efficient shortcuts to adiabaticity via fast transitionless evolutions. Moreover, we have investigated the robustness of adiabatic dynamics, standard and optimal TQD under decoherence. There always exist dynamical regimes for which generalized transitionless evolutions are more robust and therefore a preferred approach in a decohering setup.

Future perspectives

As future perspectives, there are a number of open questions to be addressed.

1)
The study introduced here on adiabatic dynamics allows us to realize further future research as detailed below.

1-a)
The consideration and influence of different reference frames in the adiabatic dynamics of the system lead us to the fundamental question of the existence of a preferable non-inertial frame. In addition, our development takes into account a single resonant field acting on the system, so that a more general approach for an arbitrary number of fields is an open question.

1-b)
The experimental implementation of the two-cell adiabatic quantum battery proposed here is a desired task to be taken in consideration as future research. The charging and discharging process of the quantum battery proposed in this thesis will be investigated in a transmon qubit superconducting system in collaboration with experimental groups. The questions to be experimentally addressed here refers to stable charging/discharging of quantum batteries based on Bell states and its robustness against decohering effects.

1-c)
When we use the terminology “quantum” in quantum devices, it is required some detailed discussion on what quantum is in such devices. For example, the quantum advantage of quantum batteries may be associated with quantum entanglement
[
86
,
87
,
88
,
89
,
90
]
and it becomes evident in multi-cell quantum batteries. Therefore, the scalability of the model proposed here is of great interest, where we can verify the real role of entanglement for the power of the charging/discharging process of the battery. Moreover, since the experimental verification of the expected quantum advantage has not been addressed so far, collaboration with experimental groups will be considered in future research.

2)
As for the generalized TQD theory, we investigated its performance in minimizing the energy cost of fast evolutions, and the pulse sequence required to implement optimal TQD. However, we can think about a number of applications that can be benefited from these particular evolutions. For example, applications of generalized TQD to holonomic quantum computing
[
160
]
, superadiabatic charging of quantum batteries through minimal intensity fields
[
62
,
63
]
and optimal superadiabatic quantum thermal engines
[
46
,
161
]
.

2-a)
In the general theory of optimal control
[
162
]
, one studies how to find a set of fields
\{\epsilon_{n}(t)\}
(pulses, for example) that drives the system along a path
|\psi(t)\rangle
in such way that we can maximize the quantity
\mathcal{O}[\psi]=\langle\psi(t)|\pazocal{O}|\psi(t)\rangle
, for some predefined observable
\pazocal{O}

[
163
]
. Then, how can we use the generalized approach of TQD in a general approach of optimal control? What is the general formalism to find optimal quantal phases from an arbitrary observable
\pazocal{O}
?

2-b)
The standard TQD has been applied to enhance performance of quantum thermal engines concerning the adiabatic version of such heat engines
[
46
,
161
,
164
]
. However, an open question here is whether this enhancement is due to the additional fields which acts on the system. As future research we will address this question by using the generalized TQD and providing a scenario where TQD and adiabatic dynamics spend a same amount of energy. Moreover, it is known that if we speed up a thermodynamics cycle in order to enhance the power, some amount of energy is thrown away as a dissipated work due to diabatic transitions
[
165
,
166
]
. In particular, it was shown that shortcuts to adiabaticity can be a good strategy to deal with this problem
[
34
]
. The open question is whether there is some optimal TQD Hamiltonian able to minimize undesired entropy production along some superadiabatic thermal cycle.

2-c)
Given the notion of adiabatic quantum battery and the role of TQD in quantum thermodynamic tasks, the proposal of superadiabatic quantum batteries based on TQD is an exciting possibility. Although the adiabatic version of such devices allows for a stable charging process, the average power is limited by the total evolution time imposed by the adiabatic theorem. Then, standard and generalized TQD positively contribute to design a high power and stable energy transference process.

2-d)
The extension for open systems of the standard version of TQD, as proposed by Demirplak and Rice, was proposed by Vacanti
et al

[
167
]
. However, different from closed system TQD, the open system TQD requires our ability of controlling both fields and decohering environment to drive the system along of an open system adiabatic path. Therefore, the extension of the generalized approach of TQD for open system is highly desirable as topic for future research.

Chapter 6
Change of frame in quantum mechanics

H
ere we present the calculations related with a change of frame in quantum mechanics, where we explore resonance phenomena and present auxiliary calculations on the validity conditions of adiabaticity from different reference frame representations.

Appendix 6.A
Spin 1/2 particle in a rotating magnetic field

Let us start from below Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H(t)=\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\omega_{1}}{2}\left[\cos(\omega t)\sigma_{x}+\sin(\omega t)\sigma_{y}\right]\text{ , }

(281)

which describes the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
particle in presence of a rotating magnetic field. The above dynamics is considered concerning the laboratory inertial frame. In such frame, the unitary system dynamics is governed by Von Neumann’s equation given by

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}(t)=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[H(t),\rho(t)]\text{ . }

(282)

Due the oscillatory behavior of the transverse field, it is convenient to rewrite the system dynamics in rotating frame provided by the time-dependent unitary transformation
R(t)=e^{i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}
. By doing that, we can implement a rotation in above equation as

\displaystyle R(t)\dot{\rho}(t)R^{\dagger}(t)=\frac{1}{i\hbar}R(t)[H(t),\rho(t)]R^{\dagger}(t)=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[R(t)H(t)R^{\dagger}(t),R(t)\rho(t)R^{\dagger}(t)]\text{ . }

(283)

where we can define the system rotated state as
\rho_{\text{R}}(t)=R(t)\rho(t)R^{\dagger}(t)
, so that

\displaystyle R(t)\dot{\rho}(t)R^{\dagger}(t)

\displaystyle=\dot{\rho}_{\text{R}}(t)-\dot{R}(t)\overbrace{R^{\dagger}(t)R(t)}^{\mathbbm{1}}\rho(t)R^{\dagger}(t)-R(t)\rho(t)\overbrace{R^{\dagger}(t)R(t)}^{\mathbbm{1}}\dot{R}^{\dagger}(t)

\displaystyle=\dot{\rho}_{\text{R}}(t)-\dot{R}(t)R^{\dagger}(t)\rho_{\text{R}}(t)-\rho_{\text{R}}(t)R(t)\dot{R}^{\dagger}(t)\text{ , }

(284)

in which we can use
\dot{R}(t)R^{\dagger}(t)=-R(t)\dot{R}^{\dagger}(t)
to get

\displaystyle R(t)\dot{\rho}(t)R^{\dagger}(t)=\dot{\rho}_{\text{R}}(t)-[\dot{R}(t)R^{\dagger}(t),\rho_{\text{R}}(t)]\text{ . }

(285)

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{\text{R}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[R(t)H(t)R^{\dagger}(t),\rho_{\text{R}}(t)]+[\dot{R}(t)R^{\dagger}(t),\rho_{\text{R}}(t)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[R(t)H(t)R^{\dagger}(t),\rho_{\text{R}}(t)]+\frac{i\hbar}{i\hbar}[\dot{R}(t)R^{\dagger}(t),\rho_{\text{R}}(t)]\text{ , }

(286)

and we conclude that the dynamics of the system in rotating frame will be given by

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{\text{R}}(t)=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[H_{\text{R}}(t),\rho_{\text{R}}(t)]\text{ , }

(287)

where the new Hamiltonian in rotating frame is
H_{\text{R}}(t)=R(t)H(t)R^{\dagger}(t)+i\hbar\dot{R}(t)R^{\dagger}(t)
. By computing the new Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{\text{R}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}}{2}e^{i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}\sigma_{z}e^{-i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}+\frac{\hbar\omega_{1}}{2}e^{i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}\left[\cos(\omega t)\sigma_{x}+\sin(\omega t)\sigma_{y}\right]e^{-i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}+i\hbar\frac{d}{dt}\left[e^{i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}\right]e^{-i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\omega_{1}}{2}\left[\cos(\omega t)e^{i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}\sigma_{x}e^{-i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}+\sin(\omega t)e^{i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}\sigma_{y}e^{-i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}\right]-\hbar\frac{\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}\text{ , }

(288)

now we can use that

\displaystyle e^{i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}\sigma_{x}e^{-i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}

\displaystyle=\cos\left(\omega t\right)\sigma_{x}-\sin\left(\omega t\right)\sigma_{y}\text{ , }

(289)

\displaystyle e^{i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}\sigma_{y}e^{-i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}

\displaystyle=\cos\left(\omega t\right)\sigma_{y}+\sin\left(\omega t\right)\sigma_{x}\text{ , }

(290)

to get the time-independent Hamiltonian

\displaystyle H_{\text{R}}(t)=H_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}-\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\omega_{1}}{2}\sigma_{x}\text{ . }

(291)

Thus, the solution of the dynamics in rotating frame reads as

\displaystyle\rho_{\text{R}}(t)=\exp\left[-\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\text{R}}t\right]\rho(0)\exp\left[\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\text{R}}t\right]\text{ , }

(292)

so that in non-rotating one we get

\displaystyle\rho(t)=R(t)\rho_{\text{R}}(t)R^{\dagger}(t)=e^{i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\text{R}}t}\rho(0)e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\text{R}}t}e^{-i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}\text{ . }

(293)

Appendix 6.B
Resonance in a two-level system driven by oscillating field

Let us consider a Landau-Zener type Hamiltonian given by

\displaystyle H(t)=\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{x}}}{2}\sin(\omega t)\sigma_{x}\text{ .}

(294)

We can rewrite Eq. (
294
) as

\displaystyle H(t)=\frac{\hbar\omega_{0}}{2}\left[\sigma_{z}+\tan\theta\sin(\omega t)\sigma_{x}\right]\text{ , }

(295)

where
\theta=\arctan[\omega_{\text{x}}/\omega_{0}]
. It is not obvious that the Hamiltonian
H(t)
in Eq. (
295
) exhibits a resonant behavior. In order to see this fact, let us define an time-dependent oscillating frame
R(t)=e^{-i\frac{\omega}{2}t\sigma_{z}}
. In this oscillating frame, the Hamiltonian is given by

\displaystyle H_{\text{R}}(t)=R(t)H(t)R^{\dagger}(t)+iR(t)\dot{R}^{\dagger}(t)=\hbar\frac{\omega_{0}-\omega}{2}\sigma_{z}+\hbar\frac{\sin(\omega t)\tan\theta}{2}\vec{\omega}_{xy}(t)\cdot\vec{\sigma}_{xy}\text{ , }

(296)

with
\vec{\omega}_{xy}(t)=\omega_{0}[\cos(\omega t)\hat{x}-\sin(\omega t)\hat{y}]
and
\vec{\sigma}_{xy}=\sigma_{x}\hat{x}+\sigma_{y}\hat{y}
.

Appendix 6.C
Proof of the theorems

Let us consider two Hamiltonians, an inertial frame Hamiltonian
H(t)
and its non-inertial counterpart
H_{\pazocal{O}}(t)
, which are related by a time-dependent unitary
\pazocal{O}(t)
. The dynamics associated with Hamiltonians
H(t)
and
H_{\pazocal{O}}(t)
are given by

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[H(t),\rho(t)]\text{ , }

(297)

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}_{\pazocal{O}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[H_{\pazocal{O}}(t),\rho_{\pazocal{O}}(t)]\text{ , }

(298)

where
H_{\pazocal{O}}(t)=\pazocal{O}(t)H(t)\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)+i\hbar\dot{\pazocal{O}}(t)\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)
and
\rho_{\pazocal{O}}(t)=\pazocal{O}(t)\rho(t)\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)
. Then, the connection between the evolved states
|\psi(t)\rangle
and
|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t)\rangle
in inertial and non-inertial frames, respectively, is given by
|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t)\rangle=\pazocal{O}(t)|\psi(t)\rangle
,
\forall t\in[t_{0},\tau]
. By considering the initial state in inertial frame given by a single eigenstate of
H(t)
, namely
|\psi(t_{0})\rangle=|E_{k}(t_{0})\rangle
, the adiabatic dynamics in this frame is written as

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle=e^{i\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\theta_{k}(\xi)d\xi}|E_{k}(t)\rangle\text{ , }

(299)

where
\theta_{k}(t)=-E_{k}(t)/\hbar+i\langle E_{k}(t)|(d/dt)|E_{k}(t)\rangle
is the adiabatic phase composed by the dynamical and geometrical phase, respectively. On the other hand, an adiabatic behavior is obtained in non-inertial frame if and only if

\displaystyle|\langle E^{\mathcal{O}}_{m}(t)|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t)\rangle|=|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t_{0})|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t_{0})\rangle|\text{ , }\forall m\,,\,\forall t\in[t_{0},\tau]\text{ . }

(300)

Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t)\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t_{0})|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t_{0})\rangle|\text{ , }

\displaystyle|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)|\pazocal{O}(t)|\psi(t)\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t_{0})|\pazocal{O}(t_{0})|\psi(t_{0})\rangle|\text{ , }

\displaystyle|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)|\pazocal{O}(t)|E_{k}(t)\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t_{0})|\pazocal{O}(t_{0})|E_{k}(t_{0})\rangle|\text{ . }

(301)

Thus, Eq. (
301
) establishes a necessary and sufficient condition to obtain an adiabatic evolution in the non-inertial frame,
assuming an adiabatic evolution in the original frame.
To conclude our proof, let us consider the converse case, where the system starts in a eigenstate of
|E^{\mathcal{O}}_{m}(t_{0})\rangle
in non-inertial frame. If the dynamics is adiabatic we write

\displaystyle|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t)\rangle=e^{i\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\theta^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(\xi)d\xi}|E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)\rangle\text{ , }

(302)

where
\theta^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)
is the adiabatic phase collected in this frame. The dynamics will be adiabatic in the inertial frame if and only if

\displaystyle|\langle E_{m}(t)|\psi(t)\rangle|=|\langle E_{m}(t_{0})|\psi(t_{0})\rangle|\text{ , }\forall m\,,\,\forall t\in[t_{0},\tau]\text{ . }

(303)

Therefore, by using the same procedure as before, we get the condition

\displaystyle|\langle E_{k}(t)|\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)|E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle E_{k}(t_{0})|\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t_{0})|E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t_{0})\rangle|\text{ , }

(304)

which is equivalent to Eq. (
301
). This ends the proof of the Theorem
2.1
. Now, let us proof the second theorem discussed in this thesis.

To this end, we need to consider a time-independent Hamiltonian
H_{\mathcal{O}}
in the non-inertial frame, so that its evolution operator can be written as
U_{\pazocal{O}}(t,t_{0})=e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\pazocal{O}}(t-t_{0})}
. Thus, we can write the dynamics in non-inertial frame as

\displaystyle|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t)\rangle=e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\pazocal{O}}(t-t_{0})}|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t_{0})\rangle\text{ . }

(305)

Moreover, assuming adiabatic dynamics in the inertial frame, we get

\displaystyle|\langle E_{k}(t)|\psi(t)\rangle|=|\langle E_{k}(t_{0})|\psi(t_{0})\rangle|\text{ . }

(306)

By using the relationship between inertial and non-inertial frames as
|\psi(t)\rangle=\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t)\rangle
, we can write

\displaystyle|\langle E_{k}(t)|\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\pazocal{O}}(t-t_{0})}|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t_{0})\rangle|=|\langle E_{k}(t_{0})|\psi(t_{0})\rangle|\text{ , }

(307)

where we have used the Eq. (
305
). Now, by taking
|\psi_{\pazocal{O}}(t_{0})\rangle=\pazocal{O}(t_{0})|\psi(t_{0})\rangle
, we obtain

\displaystyle|\langle E_{k}(t)|\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\pazocal{O}}(t-t_{0})}\pazocal{O}(t_{0})|\psi(t_{0})\rangle|=|\langle E_{k}(t_{0})|\psi(t_{0})\rangle|\text{ . }

(308)

Thus, by inserting the initial state
|\psi(t_{0})\rangle=|E_{n}(t_{0})\rangle
in Eq. (
308
), we get

\displaystyle|\langle E_{k}(t)|\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\pazocal{O}}(t-t_{0})}\pazocal{O}(t_{0})|E_{n}(t_{0})\rangle|=|\langle E_{k}(t_{0})|E_{n}(t_{0})\rangle|\text{ . }

(309)

This concludes the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

6.C.1
Application to the oscillating Hamiltonian

In order to show how the Theorem
2.1
allows us to predict the adiabatic behavior of the system driven by the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
294
), let us first consider a generic system under action of a single time-dependent oscillating field with characteristic
frequency
\omega
, whose Hamiltonian reads

\displaystyle H(\omega,t)=\hbar\omega_{0}H_{0}+\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\text{T}}(\omega,t)\text{ , }

(310)

where we consider the transverse term
\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\text{T}}(\omega,t)
as a perturbation, so that
||\omega_{0}H_{0}||\gg||\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\text{T}}(\omega,t)||
,
\forall t\in[0,\tau]
. In this case, the eigenstates
|E_{n}(t)\rangle
and energies
E_{n}(t)
of
H(\omega,t)
can be obtained as perturbation of eigenstates
|E_{n}^{0}\rangle
and energies
E_{n}^{0}
of
\hbar\omega_{0}H_{0}
as (up to a normalization coefficient)

\displaystyle|E_{n}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=|E_{n}^{0}\rangle+\pazocal{O}\left(||\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\text{T}}(\omega,t)||\right)\text{ , }

(311)

\displaystyle E_{n}(t)

\displaystyle=E_{n}^{0}+\pazocal{O}\left(||\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\text{T}}(\omega,t)||\right)\text{ . }

(312)

On the other hand, in the non-inertial frame, we have
H_{\pazocal{O}}(t)=\pazocal{O}(t)H(t)\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)+i\hbar\dot{\pazocal{O}}(t)\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)
, which yields

\displaystyle H_{\pazocal{O}}(\omega,t)=\hbar\left(\omega_{0}-\omega\right)H_{0}+\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\pazocal{O},\text{T}}(\omega,t)\text{ . }

(313)

where
H_{\pazocal{O},\text{T}}(\omega,t)=\pazocal{O}(t)H_{\text{T}}(\omega,t)\pazocal{O}^{\dagger}(t)
. Now, we separately consider two specific cases:

\bullet

Far-from resonance situation
|\omega_{0}-\omega|\gg|\omega_{\text{T}}|
: In this case, the term
\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\pazocal{O},\text{T}}(\omega,t)
in Eq. (
313
) works as a perturbation. Therefore the set of eigenvectors of
H_{\pazocal{O}}(\omega,t)
reads

\displaystyle|E^{\pazocal{O}}_{n}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=|E_{n}^{0}\rangle+\pazocal{O}\left(||\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\text{T}}(\omega,t)||\right)\text{ , }

(314)

where we have used that the energy gaps
\tilde{E}_{n}^{0}-\tilde{E}_{k}^{0}
of the Hamiltonian
\hbar\left(\omega_{0}-\omega\right)H_{0}
are identical to energy gaps
E_{n}^{0}-E_{k}^{0}
of
\hbar\omega_{0}H_{0}
and
||\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\pazocal{O},\text{T}}(\omega,t)||=||\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\text{T}}(\omega,t)||
. Thus,
from Eqs. (
311
) and (
314
) we conclude, for any eigenstate
|E_{k}{(t)}\rangle
, that

\displaystyle\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)|\pazocal{O}(t)|E_{k}(t)\rangle\approx e^{i\frac{\omega}{\omega_{0}}\frac{E_{k}^{0}}{\hbar}t}\delta_{mk}\text{ , }

(315)

so that we get
|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)|\pazocal{O}(t)|E_{k}(t)\rangle|=\text{constant}
,
\forall m
,
\forall t\in[t_{0},\tau]
.

\bullet

Resonance situation
|\omega_{0}-\omega|\ll|\omega_{\text{T}}|
: Now, we have a more subtle situation.
Firstly, we can use Eqs. (
311
) and (
312
) to write

\displaystyle\pazocal{O}(t)|E_{n}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=e^{i\frac{\omega}{\omega_{0}}\frac{E_{n}^{0}}{\hbar}t}|E_{n}^{0}\rangle+\pazocal{O}\left(||\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\text{T}}(\omega,t)||\right)\text{ , }

(316)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\theta_{n}(\xi)d\xi

\displaystyle=-\frac{E_{n}^{0}}{\hbar}(t-t_{0})+\pazocal{O}\left(||\hbar\omega_{\text{T}}H_{\text{T}}(\omega,t)||\right)\text{ , }

(317)

so that

\displaystyle\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)|\pazocal{O}(t)|E_{k}(t)\rangle\approx e^{i\frac{\omega}{\omega_{0}}\frac{E_{k}^{0}}{\hbar}t}\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)|E^{0}_{k}\rangle\text{ . }

(318)

Now, it is possible to see that if
|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)|E^{0}_{k}\rangle|=|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t_{0})|E^{0}_{k}\rangle|
,
\forall t\in[t_{0},\tau]
, then the theorem is satisfied. By applying this results to the Hamiltonian in Eq. (
296
) we can see that for
|\omega_{0}-\omega|\ll|\omega_{\text{T}}|
we get
H_{\pazocal{O}}(\omega,t)\propto\cos(\omega t)\sigma{x}-\sin(\omega t)\sigma{y}
and

\displaystyle|E^{\pazocal{O}}_{\pm}(t)\rangle=\frac{|1\rangle\pm e^{i\omega t}|0\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}\text{ . }

(319)

Since
|E^{0}_{k}\rangle=|k\rangle
, with
k=\{0,1\}
, we conclude that
|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t)|E^{0}_{k}\rangle|=|\langle E^{\pazocal{O}}_{m}(t_{0})|E^{0}_{k}\rangle|
.

Chapter 7
Experimental setup

Appendix 7.A
The Ytterbium trapped ion experimental setup

In this section we present more details on the first system used to experimentally study some theoretical results presented in this thesis. In particular, we are interested here in providing a detailed discussion about the main elements used in this thesis, so that the readers could better understand our results. For a complete discussion, we indicate the PhD thesis by Chang-Kang Hu
[
74
]
(thesis in Chinese, read it moderately!).

7.A.1
Foundations of ion trapping and qubit initialization

In general, the trapping mechanism of neutral or charged particles is dependent on the particle to be trapped, among many other factors, so here we provide a discussion on the particular system used in this thesis
[
74
]
. However, independent on the system, to trap a particle (charged or not) we use electric and magnetic fields. A general result, called Earnshaw’s theorem
[
168
]
, allows us to identify the minimum requirement to trap a particle in a stable way, more specifically, a charged particle cannot be held in a stable equilibrium by using static fields. Even though very general, the demonstration of this result is very simple and it immediately comes from the Gauss equations for electric and magnetic static fields
\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{E}=0
and
\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{B}=0
. These equations tell us that the electric/magnetic field flux going through a region (volume
V
) is null, so a charged particle under action of this field will undergoes a non-confining state.

It is reasonable to think about the trapping task for slow motion particles (less energetic), otherwise we need strong fields to trap them around a specific position. For atomic beam that comes from a heat gas, we need to reduce the beam temperature by using a procedure called Doppler cooling
[
169
,
170
]
. The Doppler cooling is a technique used to slow down the motion of each atom by using a laser beam tuned slightly below at the natural resonance frequency of the atom energy level. In a Ytterbium ion
171
Yb
+
, the relevant energy levels populated during the cooling procedure are shown in Fig.
17

[
74
,
83
]
. For the ion
171
Yb
+
the Doppler cooling is achieved through a
369.5
nm laser beam, where the cooling is obtained from the optical transition cycle
{}^{2}S_{1/2}\!\rightleftarrows\!^{2}P_{1/2}

[
83
]
. This cycle transition happens because the system at state
{}^{2}S_{1/2}
absorbs a photon and it becomes excited at the state
{}^{2}P_{1/2}
, then the atom spontaneously emits a photon in a aleatory direction and uniformly probability distribution. In particular, if the system decays to state the
{}^{2}S_{1/2}
, the system remain in cooling cycle. However, as shown in Fig.
17
, during the transition cycle
{}^{2}S_{1/2}\!\rightleftarrows\!^{2}P_{1/2}
there is a branching ratio
R
for population decay from
{}^{2}P_{1/2}
state to the
{}^{2}D_{3/2}
, where the decay probability has been theoretically studied in Ref.
[
171
]
, with experimentally verification in Refs.
[
83
,
172
]
. In order to sent the system back to the cooling cycle, a light at
935.2
nm is used to promote transitions
{}^{2}D_{3/2}\!\rightleftarrows\!^{3}D[3/2]_{1/2}
, where the system can quickly decay from
{}^{3}D[3/2]_{1/2}
to
{}^{2}S_{1/2}
. Therefore, by driving the system through a many cooling cycles process we can achieve the cooling limit (minimal temperature). This limit is known as
Doppler cooling limit
and given it predicts the minimal temperature
T_{\text{D}}=\hbar\Gamma/2k_{\text{B}}
, where
\Gamma
is a characteristic parameter associated to system (transition linewidth) and
k_{\text{B}}
is the Boltzmann’s constant
[
173
,
174
,
175
,
176
,
177
]
. For the Ytterbium ion
171
Yb
+
we have
\Gamma=2\pi\times 19.6
MHz, so that we can find
T_{\text{D}}\approx 468.5\penalty\ \mu
K. Thus, since we are interested in applications in quantum information processing, after Doppler cooling step a single ion is trapped by the radio frequency electric field.

Figure 17:
Relevant energy levels of the Ytterbium ion
171
Yb
+
used in the experimental implementations in this thesis.

After the cooling and trapping process, the qubit is initialized and then can be manipulated in order to process quantum information. Our qubit is encoded within energy subspace
{}^{2}S_{1/2}
spanned by the states
{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=0,m=0\rangle
,
{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=1,m=0\rangle
and
{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=1,m=\pm 1\rangle
, where
F
denotes the total angular momentum of the atom and
m_{F}
is its projection along the quantization axis. In absence of an external fields, the subspace
F=1
is degenerate. Therefore, when we apply an external static magnetic field
\vec{B}
the Zeeman effect in order to highlight the hyperfine structure splitting, so that an amount of energy
\delta E\propto||\vec{B}||
separates the energy eigenstates with
F=1
, as shown in Fig.
2
. Thus, we identify subspace of two states that can be used as a two-level system, i.e., as our desired qubit. The Zeeman shift in transition frequency is given by
\delta_{\text{Z}}=1.4
MHz/G and the transition frequency of the qubit reads as
\omega_{\text{s}}^{0}\approx 12.64
GHz. It is important to mention that the frequency
\omega_{\text{s}}^{0}
is smoothly affected by the external magnetic field as
\omega_{\text{s}}=\omega^{0}_{\text{s}}+\delta_{\text{2z}}
, where
\delta_{\text{2z}}\approx 310.8B^{2}
Hz is the second order Zeeman shift (
B
is the intensity of the static magnetic field in Gauss)
[
74
]
. Thus, in the experiments considered in this thesis, an additional shift about
2\pi\times 10
KHz is added. Therefore, the qubit is encoded in states
|0\rangle\!\equiv\!^{2}S_{1/2}|F=0,m=0\rangle
and
|1\rangle\!\equiv\!^{2}S_{1/2}|F=1,m=0\rangle
, where the energy gap reads as
E_{01}=\hbar\omega_{\text{s}}
.

7.A.2
Trapped ion qubit dynamics

Given the natural configuration of the system and the set of states spanned by
{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=0,m=0\rangle
and
{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=1,m=0\rangle
, with energies
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
, respectively, we can define the bare Hamiltonian for our qubit as

\displaystyle H_{0}=E_{0}|0\rangle\langle 0|+E_{1}|1\rangle\langle 1|=\frac{E_{01}}{2}\sigma_{z}=\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{s}}}{2}\sigma_{z}\text{ , }

(320)

where
\sigma_{z}=|1\rangle\langle 1|-|0\rangle\langle 0|
is the Pauli matrix. As schematically shown in Fig.
2
, by using an arbitrary waveform generator (AWG) we can manipulate the qubit
through a unitary dynamics by driving the system with an magnetic field
\vec{B}_{\text{un}}(t)=\vec{B}_{0}\cos(\omega t+\phi)
, one uses the dipole approximation to write the contribution of this interaction to energy of the system as
H_{1}=-\vec{\mu}\cdot\vec{B}_{\text{un}}(t)
, where
\vec{\mu}=\vec{\mu}_{01}|0\rangle\langle 1|+\vec{\mu}_{10}|1\rangle\langle 0|

[
124
,
178
]
. Here we use the subscript “un” to denote the field associated with a unitary dynamics, later we shall see that the same AWG can be used to design a non-unitary dynamics through a field
\vec{B}_{\text{n-un}}(t)
. Therefore, we find

\displaystyle H_{1}(t)=-\vec{\mu}_{01}\cdot\vec{B}_{0}\cos(\omega t+\phi)|0\rangle\langle 1|-\vec{\mu}_{10}\cdot\vec{B}_{0}\cos(\omega t+\phi)|1\rangle\langle 0|\text{ . }

(321)

Then, for simplicity we use that
\vec{\mu}_{01}=\vec{\mu}_{10}=\vec{\mu}
, so that the total Hamiltonian
H(t)=H_{0}+H_{1}
reads as

\displaystyle H(t)=\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{s}}}{2}\sigma_{z}+\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}\cos(\omega t+\phi)\sigma_{x}\text{ , }

(322)

where we defined the Rabi frequency
\omega_{\text{R}}=-\vec{\mu}_{01}\cdot\vec{B}_{0}/\hbar
and the Pauli matrix
\sigma_{x}=|0\rangle\langle 1|+|1\rangle\langle 0|
. Now, by using perturbation theory we can write the dynamical equation to the coefficients
c_{0}(t)
and
c_{1}(t)
associated with the evolved state

\displaystyle|\psi(t)\rangle=c_{0}(t)|0\rangle+c_{1}(t)|1\rangle\text{ . }

(323)

In fact, by writing
|\psi_{\text{I}}(t)\rangle=e^{iH_{0}t/\hbar}|\psi(t)\rangle
we get the dynamics for
|\psi_{\text{I}}(t)\rangle
as

\displaystyle|\dot{\psi}_{\text{I}}(t)\rangle=i\hbar V_{\text{I}}(t)|\psi_{\text{I}}(t)\rangle\text{ , }

(324)

with
V_{\text{I}}(t)=e^{iH_{0}t/\hbar}H_{1}(t)e^{-iH_{0}t/\hbar}
. As a first remark, note that
[H_{1}(t_{1}),H_{1}(t_{2})]=0
for any
(t_{1},t_{2})
, therefore we can also write
[V_{\text{I}}(t_{1}),V_{\text{I}}(t_{2})]=0
for any
(t_{1},t_{2})
. This tells us that the solution for the above equation reads as

\displaystyle|\psi_{\text{I}}(t)\rangle=U_{\text{I}}(t)|\psi_{\text{I}}(0)\rangle\text{ , }

(325)

with

\displaystyle U_{\text{I}}(t)=\exp\left[\frac{1}{i\hbar}\int_{0}^{t}V_{\text{I}}(\xi)d\xi\right]\text{ . }

(326)

We use that

\displaystyle V_{\text{I}}(t)

\displaystyle=e^{iH_{0}t/\hbar}H_{1}(t)e^{-iH_{0}t/\hbar}=\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left(e^{i(\omega t+\phi)}+e^{-i(\omega t+\phi)}\right)e^{iH_{0}t/\hbar}\sigma_{x}e^{-iH_{0}t/\hbar}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left(e^{i(\omega t+\phi)}+e^{-i(\omega t+\phi)}\right)e^{i\omega_{\text{s}}t/2}\left(|0\rangle\langle 1|+|1\rangle\langle 0|\right)e^{-i\omega_{\text{s}}t/2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left(e^{i(\omega t+\phi)}+e^{-i(\omega t+\phi)}\right)\left(e^{-i\omega_{\text{s}}t}|0\rangle\langle 1|+e^{i\omega_{\text{s}}t}|1\rangle\langle 0|\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left(e^{i(\omega_{-}t+\phi)}+e^{-i(\omega_{+}t+\phi)}\right)|0\rangle\langle 1|+\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left(e^{i(\omega_{+}t+\phi)}+e^{-i(\omega_{-}t+\phi)}\right)|1\rangle\langle 0|\text{ , }

(327)

where
\omega_{\pm}=\omega-\omega_{\text{s}}
. Therefore, by integrating the above expression

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}V_{\text{I}}(\xi)d\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left[\frac{-ie^{i\phi}\left(e^{i\omega_{-}t}-1\right)}{\omega_{-}}+\frac{ie^{-i\phi}\left(e^{-i\omega_{+}t}-1\right)}{\omega_{+}}\right]|0\rangle\langle 1|

\displaystyle+\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left[\frac{-ie^{i\phi}\left(e^{i\omega_{+}t}-1\right)}{\omega_{+}}+\frac{ie^{-i\phi}\left(e^{-i\omega_{-}t}-1\right)}{\omega_{-}}\right]|1\rangle\langle 0|\text{ . }

(328)

Thus, for a highly oscillating external field, we can approximate the above expression to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}V_{\text{I}}(\xi)d\xi

\displaystyle\approx\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left[\frac{-ie^{i\phi}\left(e^{i\omega_{-}t}-1\right)}{\omega_{-}}|0\rangle\langle 1|+\frac{ie^{-i\phi}\left(e^{-i\omega_{-}t}-1\right)}{\omega_{-}}|1\rangle\langle 0|\right]\text{ . }

(329)

For this reason, in our analysis we can neglect highly oscillating terms, as we shall use soon. Now, by writing the Hamiltonian
H(t)
in rotating frame by using
R(t)=e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}
we find

\displaystyle H_{R}

\displaystyle=R(t)H(t)R^{\dagger}(t)+i\hbar\dot{R}(t)R^{\dagger}(t)=\frac{\hbar\Delta}{2}\sigma_{z}+R(t)H_{1}(t)R^{\dagger}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Delta}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left(e^{i(\omega t+\phi)}+e^{-i(\omega t+\phi)}\right)\left(e^{-i\omega t}\sigma_{-}+e^{i\omega t}\sigma_{+}\right)\text{ , }

(330)

where we define
\Delta=\omega_{\text{s}}-\omega
and we already used that
e^{i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}\sigma_{x}e^{-i\omega t\sigma_{z}/2}=e^{-i\omega t}\sigma_{-}+e^{i\omega t}\sigma_{+}
, where
\sigma_{-}=|0\rangle\langle 1|
and
\sigma_{+}=\sigma_{-}^{\dagger}
. Now, by neglecting the highly oscillating terms, it is possible to proof that we get

\displaystyle H_{R}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Delta}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left(e^{i\phi}\sigma_{-}+e^{-i\phi}\sigma_{+}\right)\text{ . }

(331)

Now, by using that
\sigma_{\pm}=(1/2)(\sigma_{x}\pm i\sigma_{y})
, we find

\displaystyle H_{R}

\displaystyle=\frac{\hbar\Delta}{2}\sigma_{z}+\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{R}}}{2}\left(\cos\phi\sigma_{x}+\sin\phi\sigma_{y}\right)\text{ . }

(332)

Therefore, the above Hamiltonian can be used to implement an arbitrary dynamics of the qubit encoded as shown in Fig.
2
.

7.A.3
State detection and tomography process

To read out the outcome of some operation in the trapped ion system used in this thesis, it is used a ion fluorescence technique
[
179
]
. In Ytterbium trapped ion systems, this technique is applied by brightening the ion with light at
369.53
nm, where we promote transitions from the state
{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=1\rangle
to
{}^{2}P_{1/2}|F=0\rangle

[
83
]
. This process allows us to determinate the population in energy level
{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=1\rangle
, since the state
{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=0\rangle
is a
dark state
for the laser at
369.53
nm. After repeating this procedure many times, it is possible to accurately estimate the probability in
bright state

{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=1\rangle
and the dark one. If some population is present in state
{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=0\rangle
, few photons are
detected due to the background scattering, and the dark counting of the photomultiplier tube (a high efficiency detector used to
measure the ion fluorescence during the state detection). On the other hand, a large number of photons are scattered by the system when the system is in state
{}^{2}S_{1/2}|F=1\rangle
. These scattered photons are collected by a numerical aperture
NA=0.4
objective lens. After a processing of the captured photons, it is possible to estimate that the measurement fidelity is around
99.4\%

[
43
]
.

In terms of state tomography, the above procedure is used to estimate the values of the quantities
|\alpha|
and
|\beta|
of an unknown state
|\psi\rangle=\alpha|0\rangle+\beta|1\rangle
. Therefore, to measure the system state we need to follow the tomography standard process where we need to measure a number of different parameters in order to reconstruct the density matrix of the system
[
150
]
. In our case, the state tomography is obtained from three parameters, since an arbitrary density matrix for a two-level system can be written as in Eq. (
18
).

7.A.4
Controllable dephasing in trapped ion systems

In order to simulate the coupling of our qubit with a dephasing reservoir, it is used frequency modulation (FM) method where a Gaussian noise is introduced by mixing the field
\vec{B}_{1}(t)
. The experimental is sketched in Fig.
10
. After this process, the new magnetic field reads as (the experiment considered in this thesis sets
\phi=0
in Eq. (
332
).)

\displaystyle\vec{B}_{\text{n-un}}(t)=\vec{B}_{0}\cos\left[\omega t+C\eta(t)t\right]\text{ , }

(333)

where the noise source is encoded in the function
\eta(t)=A_{0}g(t)
, with
A
being the average amplitude of the noise,
g(t)
is a random
analog voltage signal and
C
is the modulation depth supported by the commercial microwave generator E8257D. Here we want to demonstrate that this Gaussian noise can be used to implement the dynamics given by

\displaystyle\dot{\rho}(t)=\frac{1}{i\hbar}[H(t),\rho(t)]+\gamma(t)\left[\sigma_{z}\rho(t)\sigma_{z}-\rho(t)\right]\text{ , }

(334)

with
\gamma(t)
being the desired decohering rate. To this end, we consider here the time-independent Hamiltonian
H^{\prime}=\hbar\tilde{\omega}\sigma_{x}
obtained from Eq. (
332
) with
\Delta=0
,
\omega_{\text{R}}=\tilde{\omega}
and
\phi=0
. That is, a resonant microwave with Rabi frequency
\tilde{\omega}
. Therefore, experimentally we can proof that the dynamics is given by the above equation in two different ways: i) by accompanying the dynamics of the system and ii) by computing the
process matrix

\chi
that describes the quantum process. Here we will consider both protocols.

By taking into account a particular dynamics of a system driven by the Hamiltonian
H^{\prime}
, from Eq. (
334
) we can write the Bloch equations for
\rho(t)
as (with
\gamma(t)=\gamma_{0}
)

\displaystyle\dot{r}_{x}(t)

\displaystyle=-2\gamma_{0}r_{x}(t)\text{ , }

(335)

\displaystyle\dot{r}_{y}(t)

\displaystyle=-2\gamma_{0}\left[r_{z}(t)+r_{y}(t)\right]\text{ , }

(336)

\displaystyle\dot{r}_{z}(t)

\displaystyle=2\gamma_{0}r_{y}(t)\text{ , }

(337)

whose solution for the initial condition
|\psi(0)\rangle=|0\rangle
reads as

\displaystyle r_{x}(t)

\displaystyle=0\text{ , }\quad r_{y}(t)=\frac{2\omega e^{-\gamma_{0}t}}{\sqrt{4\tilde{\omega}^{2}-\gamma_{0}}}\sin\left(t\sqrt{4\tilde{\omega}^{2}-\gamma_{0}}\right)\text{ , }

(338)

\displaystyle r_{z}(t)

\displaystyle=-e^{-\gamma_{0}t}\left[\cos\left(t\sqrt{4\tilde{\omega}^{2}-\gamma_{0}}\right)+\frac{\gamma_{0}\sin\left(t\sqrt{4\tilde{\omega}^{2}-\gamma_{0}}\right)}{\sqrt{4\tilde{\omega}^{2}-\gamma_{0}}}\right]\text{ . }

(339)

Therefore, by defining the projector
\mathcal{P}_{1}=|1\rangle\langle 1|
computing the population in state excited state
|1\rangle
we get

\displaystyle p_{1}(t)=\text{Tr}[\mathcal{P}_{1}\rho(t)]=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}e^{-\gamma_{0}t}\left[\cos\left(t\sqrt{4\tilde{\omega}^{2}-\gamma_{0}}\right)+\frac{\gamma_{0}\sin\left(t\sqrt{4\tilde{\omega}^{2}-\gamma_{0}}\right)}{\sqrt{4\tilde{\omega}^{2}-\gamma_{0}}}\right]\text{ , }

(340)

where in the limit
\gamma_{0}\rightarrow 0
we recover the well-known equation
p_{n}(t)|_{\gamma_{0}=0}=\sin^{2}(\tilde{\omega}t)
. As we can see, the behavior of
p_{n}(t)
is a damped oscillating function that converges to
1/2
in the regime
\gamma_{0}t\gg 1
, where the damping rate is dictated by the parameter
\gamma_{0}
.

Figure 18:
Dephasing rate controlled by the amplitude of noise. (
a
) Rabi oscillations between states
|0\rangle
and
|1\rangle
under different noise intensities. From top to bottom, the noise amplitude
A_{0}
is set to
0.4
V,
0.8
V,
1.2
V,
1.6
V and
2.0
V, with the corresponding damping rates
\gamma
are approximately
180
Hz,
649
Hz,
1391
Hz,
2441
Hz and
3785
Hz, respectively. (
b
) Dephasing rate as a function of the noise amplitude. Points are measured data. A linear fit is obtained. Without driving noise (noise amplitude is zero), the dephasing rate of the qubit is fitted as 3.03 Hz, which is caused by the magnetic fluctuation in the laboratory.

To show how to simulate the non-unitary dynamics in Eq. (
334
), the experimental realization it was considered with the parameter
\tilde{\omega}=2\pi\times 8.44
KHz and the modulation depth
C
as
96.00
KHz/V, here both parameters
\tilde{\omega}
and
C
are kept fixed for all the experiments. Thus, by adjusting the amplitude noise
A_{0}
, different dynamics were implemented. We experimentally evaluated the quantity
p_{1}(t)
to get the Rabi oscillations of the system for each dynamics during a total time interval of
\Delta t_{\text{exp}}=0.5
ms. The result are shown in Fig.
18
a
for the Rabi oscillations between states
|0\rangle
and
|1\rangle
. For different values of the amplitude
A_{0}
, it is possible to see different behaviors, each one of them associated with distinct values of
\gamma_{0}
. Moreover, it is possible to experimentally evaluate the relation between the dephasing rate
\gamma
and the noise amplitude
A_{0}
, where we used the graph in Fig.
18
b
to this end. By evaluating the square root of
\gamma
we find a linear behavior as
A_{0}
increases, so through the linear regression method we find (approximately)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\gamma}=29.806861A_{0}+1.739986\text{ , }

(341)

and this show that a perfect control of decoherence rate in Eq. (
334
) is obtained from a precise control of amplitude noise.

As a second, and complementary, analysis of the decohering dynamics it was used the tomography process to reconstruct the process matrix
\chi
. Let us now give brief introduction to the matrix
\chi
used to completely describe a physical process. A general physical process is adequately characterized by the equation
[
150
]

\displaystyle\rho(0)\rightarrow\rho(x)=\pazocal{E}_{x}[\rho(0)]=\sum\nolimits_{n=1}^{N}E_{n}\rho(0)E_{n}^{\dagger}\text{ , }

(342)

where
E_{n}
is a complete set of
N
operation elements that satisfy
\sum_{n=1}^{N}E_{n}^{\dagger}E_{n}=\mathbbm{1}
, because
\pazocal{E}_{x}[\bullet]
should be a trace-preserving operation. In general we have a specific set
\{E_{n}\}
for each process considered, so for this reason it is convenient to consider a fixed basis of
K
operators
F_{n}^{k}
so that we can write
E_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}e_{nk}\bar{E}_{k}
. Therefore, we get

\displaystyle\pazocal{E}_{x}[\rho(0)]=\sum\nolimits_{n=1}^{N}\left(\sum\nolimits_{k=1}^{K}e_{nk}\bar{E}_{k}\right)\rho(0)\left(\sum\nolimits_{l=1}^{K}e^{\ast}_{nl}\bar{E}^{\dagger}_{l}\right)=\sum\nolimits_{k,l=1}^{K}\chi_{kl}\bar{E}_{k}\rho(0)\bar{E}^{\dagger}_{l}\text{ . }

(343)

Figure 19:
Process matrix obtained by process tomography, where we plot the real and imaginary parts of
\chi
obtained from the experimental measured data (
\chi_{\text{exp}}
) and numerical simulation (
\chi_{\text{the}}
).

Therefore, since the set is early defined, the above equation tells that the process is characterized by a matrix with elements
\chi_{kl}
. Thus, by following this process we adopted the reference basis for a single-qubit tomography process as
\bar{E}_{1}=\mathbbm{1}
,
\bar{E}_{2}=\sigma_{x}
,
\bar{E}_{3}=\sigma_{y}
,
\bar{E}_{4}=\sigma_{z}
in the case where the dynamics is simulated with
\gamma=2.5
KHz, where we set the total evolution time as
0.24
ms (here, the noise amplitude is 1.62 V and the modulation depth is 96.00 KHz/V). The resulting estimated process matrix is shown in Fig.
19
. Then, the fidelity between the simulated and desired process can be obtained by evaluating the fidelity between theoretical and experimental process matrices defined as

\displaystyle\pazocal{F}({\chi_{\text{exp}}},{\chi_{\text{the}}})=\left[\text{Tr}\sqrt{\sqrt{\chi_{\text{exp}}}\text{ }\chi_{\text{the}}\sqrt{\chi_{\text{exp}}}}\right]^{2}\text{ . }

(344)

For example, when the amplitude of the noise is set to
1.54
V, the process fidelities are measured as
\pazocal{F}_{t_{1}}=99.27\%
,
\pazocal{F}_{t_{2}}=99.50\%
,
\pazocal{F}_{t_{3}}=99.72\%
,
\pazocal{F}_{t_{4}}=99.86\%
and
\pazocal{F}_{t_{5}}=99.87\%
, at times
t_{1}=0.08
ms,
t_{2}=0.16
ms,
t_{3}=0.24
ms,
t_{4}=0.32
ms and
t_{5}=0.40
ms, respectively. Thus, the dephasing channel can be precisely controlled as desired and it can support the scheme to implement the time-dependent dephasing in experiment. A point to be highlighted here is the case where
A_{0}=0
, situation in which the system evolves under its natural dephasing rate
\gamma_{\text{nd}}
. From Eq. (
341
) one finds that
\gamma_{\text{nd}}\approx 1.74^{2}\approx 3.03
Hz. Thus, we can see that, if we change the parameter
A
, which we can done with high controllability, the quantity
\gamma
can be efficiently controlled. On the other hand, if we need a time-dependent rate
\gamma(t)
, we just need to consider a way to vary
A_{0}
as a function
A(t)
. To this end, we use a second channel (CH2) of the AWG to perform amplitude modulation (AM) of the Gaussian noise. The temporal dependence of
A(t)
is achieved by programming the channel (CH2) to change the noise amplitude along the evolution.

Appendix 7.B
Nuclear Magnetic Resonance experimental setup

In NMR experimental implementations for quantum information processing, we encode the quantum bit into degree of freedom associated with the nuclear spin of atoms in molecules. The NMR qubits used in this thesis are the carbon nuclei (
13
C) and the hydrogen nuclei (
1
H). However, independent on the nucleon used in our discussion, it is possible to derive the Hamiltonian used to drive the system and, consequently, implement quantum gates on the qubits. By applying a strong static magnetic field
\vec{B}_{0}=B_{0}\hat{\text{z}}
along some direction (let’s say the direction
Z
), we are choosing an quantization axes and different the spin states (spin up
|\!\!\uparrow\rangle
and spin down
|\!\!\downarrow\rangle
) of the nuclei set a energy splitting of energy given by
\Delta E=\hbar\omega_{\text{L}}
, so that we can write the internal Hamiltonian as

H_{\text{Zee}}=\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{L}}}{2}\left(|\!\!\uparrow\rangle\langle\uparrow\!\!|-|\!\!\downarrow\rangle\langle\downarrow\!\!|\right)=\frac{\hbar\omega_{\text{L}}}{2}\sigma_{z}=\hbar\omega_{\text{L}}I_{z}\text{ , }

(345)

called Zeeman Hamiltonian due to the Zeeman interaction occurring between the magnetic dipole moment of the nuclei and the local magnetic fields. The quantity
\omega_{\text{L}}
is the Larmor frequency and it is a characteristic parameter of the nuclei used in experiment; it is associated with the
gyromagnetic ratio
of the nucleus
\gamma_{0}
as
\omega_{\text{L}}=-\gamma_{0}B_{0}
.

Therefore, the qubit is encoded in states
|\!\!\uparrow\rangle
and
|\!\!\downarrow\rangle
as
|0\rangle=|\!\!\downarrow\rangle
and
|1\rangle=|\!\!\uparrow\rangle
. The qubit is driven by an transverse rotating field called
radio-frequency
(RF) field
\vec{B}_{\text{rf}}(t)
given by

\vec{B}_{\text{rf}}(t)=B_{1}\left[\cos(\omega t)\hat{\text{x}}+\sin(\omega t)\hat{\text{y}}\right]\text{ , }

(346)

where
\omega
is the rotating frequency of the field
\vec{B}_{\text{rf}}(t)
. Therefore, the single-qubit Hamiltonian is a combination of the Zeeman and RF Hamiltonian, this gives

H(t)=\hbar\omega_{0}I_{z}+\hbar\omega_{1}\left[\cos(\omega t)I_{y}+\sin(\omega t)I_{y}\right]\text{ , }

(347)

with
I_{j}=(1/2)\sigma_{j}
and
\omega_{1}=-\gamma_{0}B_{1}
. In general, since the field
|\vec{B}_{1}|
is much less intense than
|\vec{B}_{0}|
, it is convenient to consider the qubit dynamics in rotating frame as done in Appendix
6.A
. By doing that, we find the driven Hamiltonian in rotating frame as

H=\hbar(\omega_{0}-\omega)I_{z}+\hbar\omega_{1}I_{x}\text{ . }

(348)

where the term
H_{\text{Zee}}^{\text{eff}}=\hbar(\omega_{0}-\omega)I_{z}
is the effective Zeeman Hamiltonian. Therefore, when we set
\omega_{0}=\omega
we find the resulting Hamiltonian in rotating frame at resonance as

H_{x}=\hbar\omega_{1}I_{x}\text{ . }

(349)

Then, by letting the system evolve under this Hamiltonian we get the evolution operator given by

U_{x}(\Delta t)=\exp\left(-\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{x}\Delta t\right)=\exp\left(-i\omega_{1}I_{x}\Delta t\right)=R_{x}(\varpi)\text{ , }

(350)

where we defined the angle
\varpi=\omega_{1}\Delta t
, with
\Delta t
being the pulse duration of the RF field. Therefore, we can adjust the time interval of each RF pulse in order to select an arbitrary value for
\varpi
. After such pulse, the system is again governed by the effective Zeeman Hamiltonian, through a “free evolution”. In this case, the evolution operator becomes

U_{\text{f}}(\Delta t)=\exp\left(-\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\text{Zee}}^{\text{eff}}\Delta t\right)=\exp\left(-i(\omega_{0}-\omega)I_{z}\Delta t\right)=R_{z}(\zeta)\text{ , }

(351)

where we define the angle
\zeta=(\omega_{0}-\omega)\Delta t
. Therefore, by using pulses
R_{x}(\varpi)
and free evolution
R_{z}(\zeta)
we can implement any single-qubit rotation
[
152
]
.

In addition, the Hamiltonian that describes a two-qubit system here is obtained by considering the nuclear Zeeman effect, chemical shifts (due to the orbital motion of nearby electrons) and
J
-coupling (interaction between the
nuclear magnetic dipole moments of neighbor nuclei), which are represented by the following secular Hamiltonian (for spin-
\frac{1}{2}
nuclei)

\pazocal{H}=\hbar\omega_{0}^{(1)}I_{z}^{(1)}+\hbar\omega_{0}^{(2)}I_{z}^{(2)}+2\pi\hbar J_{12}I_{z}^{(1)}I_{z}^{(2)}

(352)

where
I_{z}^{(n)}=(1/2)\sigma_{z}^{(n)}
is the nuclear spin operators along the quantization axes for the
n
-th nuclei. Therefore, since the
J
-coupling can be used to implement any controlled phase gate
[
31
]
, it is possible to implement universal quantum computation with these number of single and two-qubit gates discussed here.

7.B.1
Pulses composition for adiabatic and TQD quantum gates

From a Trotter decomposition approach for a time-dependent quantum Hamiltonian (see, e.g., Ref.
[
150
]
), we use the pulse sequence
shown in Fig.
16
a
to implement each Hamiltonian in Eqs. (
280a
), (
280b
) and (
280c
).
The algorithm is composed by rotations
(\vartheta)_{\varphi_{n}}
and free evolutions. The rotations are implemented of an angle
\vartheta
around a direction
\hat{\varphi}_{n}=\cos\varphi_{n}\hat{\text{x}}+\sin\varphi_{n}\hat{\text{y}}
, whose the rotation operator reads
[
150
]

(\vartheta)_{\varphi_{n}}=\exp\left[-\frac{i\vartheta}{2}\hat{\varphi}_{n}\cdot\vec{\sigma}\right]\text{ , }

(353)

where
\vec{\sigma}=\sigma_{x}\hat{\text{x}}+\sigma_{y}\hat{\text{y}}+\sigma_{z}\hat{\text{z}}
, with
\sigma_{n}
being the Pauli matrices. Each direction
\varphi_{n}
of the rotations in Fig.
16
a
-I are obtained from

\varphi_{1}=\pi+\chi_{n}\text{ , }\quad\varphi_{2}=\varphi_{4}=\chi_{n}\text{ , }\quad\varphi_{3}=\frac{\pi}{2}+\chi_{n}\text{ , }

(354)

with

\chi_{n}=\sum_{\ell=n+1}^{N}\left[-\frac{4\pi\nu\tau}{N-1}\cos\left(\pi\frac{t_{k}}{\tau}\right)+\frac{\pi}{2}\right]\text{ . }

(355)

The free evolution used in circuits of the Figs.
16
a
-I and
16
a
-II appear as a consequence of the natural spin evolution
of the nuclei in our chloroform molecule, so that the evolution operator reads as

U_{\text{free}}(\Delta t)=e^{-\frac{i}{\hbar}H_{\text{free}}\Delta t}\text{ , }

(356)

where

\displaystyle H_{\text{free}}=\hbar\frac{J}{2}\sigma_{z}^{(\text{C})}\sigma_{z}^{(\text{H})}\text{ . }

(357)

In our experiment, the interaction between the spins is constant with strength
J=215
Hz, so that we need to manipulate some additional parameters in order to implement different values of
\tau
of the interaction term in Eqs. (
280
). To this end, we use a free evolution for coupled spins so that we can map the parameter
\tau
from two parameter
J
and the time intervals
\Delta t
of each free evolutions. This relation is obtained as

\displaystyle\Delta t_{1,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau}{2(N-1)}\left[\frac{4\nu}{J}\sin\left(\pi\frac{t_{k}}{\tau}\right)+1\right]\text{ , }\quad

\displaystyle\Delta t_{2,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau}{N-1}-\Delta t_{1,n}\text{ , }\quad

\displaystyle\Delta t_{3,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau}{2(N-1)}\left(\frac{1}{J\tau}+1\right)\text{ , }\quad

\displaystyle\Delta t_{4,n}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tau}{N-1}-\Delta t_{3,n}\text{ . }

(358)

For Fig.
16
a
-III, the Hamiltonian is constant
and the time interval is then given by

\Delta t_{1}=\frac{J\tau+1}{2J}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Delta t_{2}=\tau-\Delta t_{1}\text{ . }

(359)

Therefore, we can choose the value of transitionless total evolution time
\tau
according with the parameter
\Delta t
, for a constant value of
J
.

Chapter 8
Adiabatic dynamics in open system

Appendix 8.A
Evolution operator for adiabatic dynamics in open system

Here we study some properties of the evolution operator
\pazocal{U}(t,t_{0})
as derived in Eq. (
135
). First, given the evolution operator
\pazocal{U}(t,t_{0})
, let us show a necessary and sufficient condition to find
\pazocal{U}^{-1}(t,t_{0})
so that
\pazocal{U}(t,t_{0})\pazocal{U}^{-1}(t,t_{0})=\mathbbm{1}
. To this, let us define

\displaystyle\pazocal{U}^{-1}(t,t_{0})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}^{-1}(t,t_{0})\text{ , }

(360)

with each operator
\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}^{-1}(t,t_{0})
obtained from

\displaystyle\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}^{-1}(t,t_{0})=e^{-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\lambda_{\alpha}(\xi)d\xi}\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{m_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\tilde{u}_{n_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{m_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{n_{\alpha}}(t)|\text{ , }

(361)

with parameters
\tilde{u}_{n_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}}(t)
to be determined. This definition is convenient because we can write

\displaystyle\pazocal{U}_{\beta}(t,t_{0})\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}^{-1}(t,t_{0})=\delta_{\alpha\beta}\pazocal{U}_{\beta}(t,t_{0})\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}^{-1}(t,t_{0})\text{ , }

(362)

where we use the orthogonality relation between right- and left-hand side quasi-eigenvectors. Now, we write

\displaystyle\pazocal{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=\pazocal{U}(t,t_{0})\pazocal{U}^{-1}(t,t_{0})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N}\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}(t,t_{0})\pazocal{U}_{\beta}^{-1}(t,t_{0})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}(t,t_{0})\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}^{-1}(t,t_{0})\text{ , }

(363)

where we already used the Eq. (
362
). Thus

\displaystyle\pazocal{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\left[\left(\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{m_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{n_{\alpha}}(t)\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{m_{\alpha}}(t_{0})|\right)\left(\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{k_{\alpha}}(t)|\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\left[\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{m_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\alpha}m_{\alpha}}(t)\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{n_{\alpha}}(t)\rangle\rangle\left(\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{m_{\alpha}}(t_{0})|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\right)\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{k_{\alpha}}(t)|\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\left[\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\alpha}j_{\alpha}}(t)\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{n_{\alpha}}(t)\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{k_{\alpha}}(t)|\right]\text{ . }

(364)

Now, computing the matrix elements
\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\eta}^{p_{\eta}}(t)|\pazocal{A}_{1}|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{m_{\nu}}(t)\rangle\rangle
, we get (to simplify the notation, from now on we will omit the time-dependence of the coefficients
u
and
\tilde{u}
)

\displaystyle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\eta}^{p_{\eta}}(t)|\pazocal{A}|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{m_{\nu}}(t)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\left[\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\alpha}j_{\alpha}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\eta}^{p_{\eta}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{n_{\alpha}}(t)\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{k_{\alpha}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{m_{\nu}}(t)\rangle\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\left[\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\alpha}j_{\alpha}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}\delta_{p_{\eta}n_{\alpha}}\delta_{k_{\alpha}m_{\nu}}\delta_{\eta\alpha}\delta_{\alpha\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\left[\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}u_{p_{\eta}j_{\alpha}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}m_{\nu}}\delta_{\eta\alpha}\delta_{\alpha\nu}\right]=\delta_{\eta\nu}\sum_{j_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}u_{p_{\eta}j_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\nu}m_{\nu}}\text{ . }

(365)

Therefore, to obtain
\pazocal{A}_{1}=\mathbbm{1}
the coefficients need to satisfy

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}u_{p_{\nu}j_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\nu}m_{\nu}}=\delta_{p_{\nu}m_{\nu}}\text{ . }

(366)

A second important property of the operator
\pazocal{U}(t,t_{0})
is related with the Jordan block form of the Lindbladian. To proof it, let us consider a operator
\pazocal{A}_{2}
given by
\pazocal{A}_{2}=\pazocal{U}^{-1}(t,t_{0})\mathbbm{L}(t)\pazocal{U}(t,t_{0})
, so we have

\displaystyle\pazocal{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=\pazocal{U}^{-1}(t,t_{0})\mathbbm{L}(t)\pazocal{U}(t,t_{0})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N}\underbrace{\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}^{-1}(t,t_{0})\mathbbm{L}(t)\pazocal{U}_{\beta}(t,t_{0})}_{\pazocal{A}_{2}^{\alpha\beta}}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\sum_{\beta=1}^{N}\pazocal{A}_{2}^{\alpha\beta}\text{ . }

(367)

Now let us analyze the each matrix
\pazocal{A}_{2}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\pazocal{A}_{2}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\pazocal{U}_{\alpha}^{-1}(t,t_{0})\mathbbm{L}(t)\pazocal{U}_{\beta}(t,t_{0})

\displaystyle=e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\lambda_{\beta}(\xi)-\lambda_{\alpha}(\xi)d\xi}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{n_{\beta}=1}^{N_{\beta}}\sum_{p_{\beta}=1}^{N_{\beta}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\beta}p_{\beta}}\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{k_{\alpha}}(t)|\mathbbm{L}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}^{n_{\beta}}(t)\rangle\rangle|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\beta}^{p_{\beta}}(t_{0})|

\displaystyle=e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\lambda_{\beta}(\xi)-\lambda_{\alpha}(\xi)d\xi}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{n_{\beta}=1}^{N_{\beta}}\sum_{p_{\beta}=1}^{N_{\beta}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\beta}p_{\beta}}\lambda_{\alpha}(t)\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{k_{\alpha}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}^{n_{\beta}}(t)\rangle\rangle|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\beta}^{p_{\beta}}(t_{0})|

\displaystyle+e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\lambda_{\beta}(\xi)-\lambda_{\alpha}(\xi)d\xi}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{n_{\beta}=1}^{N_{\beta}}\sum_{p_{\beta}=1}^{N_{\beta}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\beta}p_{\beta}}\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{k_{\alpha}}(t)|\pazocal{D}_{\beta}^{(n_{\beta}-1)}(t)\rangle\rangle|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\beta}^{p_{\beta}}(t_{0})|\text{ , }

where we already used the quasi-eigenvalue equation. Thus, due orthogonality of the basis we write

\displaystyle\pazocal{A}_{2}^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\lambda_{\beta}(\xi)-\lambda_{\alpha}(\xi)d\xi}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{n_{\beta}=1}^{N_{\beta}}\sum_{p_{\beta}=1}^{N_{\beta}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\beta}p_{\beta}}\lambda_{\alpha}(t)\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{k_{\alpha}n_{\beta}}|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\beta}^{p_{\beta}}(t_{0})|

\displaystyle+e^{\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\lambda_{\beta}(\xi)-\lambda_{\alpha}(\xi)d\xi}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{n_{\beta}=1}^{N_{\beta}}\sum_{p_{\beta}=1}^{N_{\beta}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\beta}p_{\beta}}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{k_{\alpha}(n_{\beta}-1)}|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\beta}^{p_{\beta}}(t_{0})|\text{ , }

and due the
\delta_{\alpha\beta}
, we can write

\displaystyle\pazocal{A}_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{p_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}}\lambda_{\alpha}(t)\delta_{k_{\alpha}n_{\alpha}}|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}(t_{0})|

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{k_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{p_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}k_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}}\delta_{k_{\alpha}(n_{\alpha}-1)}|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}(t_{0})|

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{p_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\left(\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}n_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}}\lambda_{\alpha}(t)+\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}(n_{\alpha}-1)}u_{n_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}}\right)|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}(t_{0})|\text{ . }

(368)

By computing the matrix elements of
\pazocal{A}_{2}
in basis
\{|\pazocal{D}_{\alpha}^{j_{\alpha}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle,\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\alpha}^{p_{\alpha}}(t_{0})|\}
, we get

\displaystyle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\eta}^{g_{\eta}}(t_{0})|\pazocal{A}_{2}|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{l_{\nu}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{N}\sum_{j_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{n_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\sum_{p_{\alpha}=1}^{N_{\alpha}}\left(\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}n_{\alpha}}u_{n_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}}\lambda_{\alpha}(t)+\tilde{u}_{j_{\alpha}(n_{\alpha}-1)}u_{n_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}}\right)\delta_{\eta\alpha}\delta_{g_{\eta}j_{\alpha}}\delta_{\alpha\nu}\delta_{p_{\alpha}l_{\nu}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{\eta}=1}^{N_{\eta}}\left(\tilde{u}_{g_{\eta}n_{\eta}}u_{n_{\eta}l_{\nu}}\lambda_{\eta}(t)+\tilde{u}_{g_{\eta}(n_{\eta}-1)}u_{n_{\eta}l_{\nu}}\right)\delta_{\eta\nu}\text{ . }

(369)

As a first result, we can see that
\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\eta}^{g_{\eta}}(t_{0})|\pazocal{A}_{2}|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{l_{\nu}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle=0
for any element outside some block, it means that
\pazocal{A}_{2}
is block diagonal in this basis. Thus, the elements of a single block are

\displaystyle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\nu}^{g_{\nu}}(t_{0})|\pazocal{A}_{2}|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{l_{\nu}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\nu}(t)\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{g_{\nu}n_{\nu}}u_{n_{\nu}l_{\nu}}+\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{g_{\nu}(n_{\nu}-1)}u_{n_{\nu}l_{\nu}}\text{ . }

(370)

The above equation provides us some new conditions on the coefficients which allows us to identify the elements
\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\nu}^{g_{\nu}}(t_{0})|\pazocal{A}_{2}|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{l_{\nu}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle
as the elements of the Jordan-Block form for
\mathbbm{L}(t)
. In fact, if we impose that

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{l_{\nu}(n_{\nu}-1)}u_{n_{\nu}l_{\nu}}=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{l_{\nu}n_{\nu}}u_{n_{\nu}l_{\nu}}=1\text{ , }

(371)

we find the diagonal elements of a single block as

\displaystyle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\nu}^{l_{\nu}}(t_{0})|\pazocal{A}_{2}|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{l_{\nu}}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\nu}(t)\text{ . }

(372)

It is important to remark that the second condition in Eq. (
371
) does not imply in condition in Eq. (
366
). In case we are interested in get a Jordan block decomposition, the first element after a diagonal element should be
1
, that is, a third condition should provides

\displaystyle\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\nu}^{l_{\nu}}(t_{0})|\pazocal{A}_{2}|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{l_{\nu}+1}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle=1\quad\text{ and }\quad\langle\langle\pazocal{E}_{\nu}^{N_{\nu}}(t_{0})|\pazocal{A}_{2}|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{N_{\nu}+1}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle=0\text{ , }

(373)

where the last condition is associated with last element in Jordan block. However, the last equation is automatically satisfied because
|\pazocal{D}_{\nu}^{N_{\nu}+1}(t_{0})\rangle\rangle=0
, for construction. Therefore, we just need to have

\displaystyle\lambda_{\nu}(t)\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{l_{\nu}n_{\nu}}u_{n_{\nu}(l_{\nu}+1)}+\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{l_{\nu}(n_{\nu}-1)}u_{n_{\nu}(l_{\nu}+1)}=1\text{ , }

(374)

whose a possible solution is

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{l_{\nu}n_{\nu}}u_{n_{\nu}(l_{\nu}+1)}=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\sum_{n_{\nu}=1}^{N_{\nu}}\tilde{u}_{l_{\nu}(n_{\nu}-1)}u_{n_{\nu}(l_{\nu}+1)}=1\text{ . }

(375)

Chapter 9
Complementary discussion on transitionless quantum driving

Appendix 9.A
Transitionless quantum driving in closed systems

As a first task, let us proof the Theorem
4.1
. To this end, consider a
D
-dimensional quantum system and adopt as a measure of energy cost the Hamiltonian Hilbert-Schmidt norm, which reads

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\text{tqd}}\left(\tau\right)=\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau}\sqrt{\text{Tr}\left[{H_{\text{tqd}}}^{2}\left(t\right)\right]}\text{ }dt\text{ ,}

(376)

Then, we obtain

\displaystyle H_{\text{tqd}}^{2}\left(t\right)=\hbar^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{D}\left[\frac{}{}|\dot{E}_{n}(t)\rangle\langle\dot{E}_{n}(t)|+\theta_{n}^{2}\left(t\right)|E_{n}(t)\rangle\langle E_{n}(t)|+i\theta_{n}\left(t\right)\left(|E_{n}(t)\rangle\langle\dot{E}_{n}(t)|-|\dot{E}_{n}(t)\rangle\langle E_{n}(t)|\right)\right]\text{ .}

(377)

By taking the trace of
H_{\text{tqd}}^{2}\left(t\right)
in Eq. (
377
), we have

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left[H_{\text{tqd}}^{2}\left(t\right)\right]=\sum_{m=1}^{D}\langle E_{m}(t)|H_{\text{tqd}}^{2}\left(t\right)|E_{m}(t)\rangle=\hbar^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{D}\left[\langle\dot{E}_{n}(t)|\dot{E}_{n}(t)\rangle+\theta_{n}^{2}\left(t\right)+2i\theta_{n}\left(t\right)\langle\dot{E}_{n}(t)|E_{n}(t)\rangle\right]\text{ .}

(378)

Then

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\text{\text{tqd}}}\left(\tau\right)=\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau}\sqrt{\sum_{n=1}^{D}\left[\langle\dot{E}_{n}(t)|\dot{E}_{n}(t)\rangle+\Gamma_{n}(\theta_{n})\right]}\text{ }dt\text{ ,}

(379)

where we have
\Gamma_{n}(\theta_{n})=\theta_{n}^{2}\left(t\right)+2i\theta_{n}\left(t\right)\langle\dot{E}_{n}(t)|E_{n}(t)\rangle
. We can now find out the functions
\theta_{n}\left(t\right)

that minimize the energy cost in transitionless evolutions. For this
end, we minimize the quantity
\Sigma_{\text{tqd}}\left(\tau\right)

for the Hamiltonian
H_{\text{tqd}}\left(t\right)
with
respect to parameters
\theta_{n}\left(t\right)
, where we will adopt it
being independents. By evaluating
\partial_{\theta_{n}}\Sigma\left(\tau\right)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta_{n}}\Sigma_{\text{tqd}}\left(\tau\right)=\frac{1}{2\tau}\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{\partial_{\theta_{n}}\{\text{Tr}[H_{\text{tqd}}^{2}\left(t\right)]\}}{\sqrt{\text{Tr}\left[{H}_{\text{tqd}}^{2}\left(t\right)\right]}}\text{ }dt\text{ .}

(380)

We then impose
\partial_{\theta_{n}}\{
Tr
[H_{\text{tqd}}^{2}\left(t\right)]\}=0
for all time
t\in\left[0,\tau\right]
,
which ensures
\partial_{\theta_{n}}\Sigma_{\text{tqd}}\left(\tau\right)=0
.
Thus, by using Eq. (
378
), we write

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta_{n}}\{\text{Tr}[H_{\text{tqd}}^{2}\left(t\right)]\}=2\theta_{n}\left(t\right)+2i\langle\dot{E}_{n}(t)|E_{n}(t)\rangle=0\text{ .}

(381)

This implies

\displaystyle\theta_{n}\left(t\right)=\theta^{\text{min}}_{n}\left(t\right)=-i\langle\dot{E}_{n}(t)|E_{n}(t)\rangle\text{ .}

(382)

From the second derivative analysis, it follows that the choice for
\theta_{n}\left(t\right)
as in Eq. (
382
)
necessarily minimizes the energy cost, namely,
\partial^{2}_{\theta_{n}}\Sigma_{\text{tqd}}\left(\tau\right)|_{\theta_{n}=\theta^{\text{min}}_{n}}>0
, which concludes the proof.

Now, to proof the Theorem
4.2
we need to take the time derivative of the Hamiltonian
H_{\text{SA}}(t)
, where we obtain

\displaystyle\dot{H}_{\text{SA}}\left(t\right)=i\sum_{n=1}^{D}\frac{d}{dt}\left[\frac{{}}{{}}|\dot{E}_{n}(t)\rangle\langle E_{n}(t)|+i\theta_{n}\left(t\right)|E_{n}(t)\rangle\langle E_{n}(t)|\frac{{}}{{}}\right]\text{ .}

(383)

Then, the matrix elements of
\dot{H}_{\text{SA}}\left(t\right)

in the eigenbasis
\left\{|E_{m}(t)\rangle\right\}
of the Hamiltonian
H_{0}(t)
read

\displaystyle\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{H}_{\text{SA}}\left(t\right)|E_{m}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=i\langle E_{k}(t)|\ddot{E}_{m}(t)\rangle+i\sum_{n=1}^{D}\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{E}_{n}(t)\rangle\langle\dot{E}_{n}(t)|E_{m}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle-\left[\dot{\theta}_{k}\left(t\right)\delta_{km}+\theta_{m}\left(t\right)\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{E}_{m}(t)\rangle+\theta_{k}\left(t\right)\langle\dot{E}_{k}(t)|E_{m}(t)\rangle\right].

(384)

Now, in second term of the above equation we can use that
\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{E}_{n}(t)\rangle=-\langle\dot{E}_{k}(t)|E_{n}(t)\rangle
, to write
\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{E}_{n}(t)\rangle\langle\dot{E}_{n}(t)|E_{m}(t)\rangle=\langle\dot{E}_{k}(t)|E_{n}(t)\rangle\langle E_{n}(t)|\dot{E}_{m}(t)\rangle
and thus

\displaystyle\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{H}_{\text{SA}}\left(t\right)|E_{m}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=i\frac{d}{d{t}}\left[\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{E}_{m}(t)\rangle\right]

\displaystyle-\left\{\dot{\theta}_{k}\left(t\right)\delta_{km}+\left[\theta_{m}\left(t\right)-\theta_{k}\left(t\right)\right]\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{E}_{m}(t)\rangle\right\}\text{ .}

(385)

For
k=m
in Eq. (
385
),
we impose the vanishing of the diagonal elements of
\dot{H}_{\text{SA}}(t)
, namely,

\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{H}_{\text{SA}}\left(t\right)|E_{k}(t)\rangle=0
. This yields

\displaystyle\dot{\theta}_{m}\left(t\right)=i\frac{d}{dt}\left[\langle E_{m}(t)|\dot{E}_{m}(t)\rangle\right]\text{ ,}

(386)

On the other hand, for
k\neq m
in Eq. (
385
), we now impose the vanishing
of the off-diagonal elements of
\dot{H}_{\text{SA}}(t)
,
namely,
\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{H}_{\text{SA}}\left(t\right)|E_{m}(t)\rangle=0

(k\neq m)
. This yields

\displaystyle i\frac{d}{dt}\left[\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{E}_{m}(t)\rangle\right]=\left[\theta_{m}\left(t\right)-\theta_{k}\left(t\right)\right]\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{E}_{m}(t)\rangle\ \ \text{ for }k\neq m\text{ .}

(387)

By taking
\langle E_{m}(t)|\dot{E}_{m}(t)\rangle\equiv c_{mm}
in Eq. (
386
), with
c_{mm}

denoting by hypothesis complex constants, we get

\theta_{m}\left(t\right)=\theta_{m}\left(0\right)\equiv\theta_{m}
, namely,
\theta_{m}\left(t\right)
is
a constant function
\forall

m
. Moreover, by using that

\langle E_{k}(t)|\dot{E}_{m}(t)\rangle\equiv c_{km}
in Eq. (
387
), with
c_{km}
denoting

nonvanishing
complex constants, we obtain
\theta_{k}=\theta_{m}
,
\forall

k,m
.
If
c_{km}=0
, then
\theta_{k}
and
\theta_{m}
are not necessarily equal, but Eq. (
387
)
will also be satisfied by this choice. Therefore, it follows that
\theta_{m}(t)
can be simply taken as

\displaystyle\theta_{m}(t)=\theta_{m}=\theta\,\,\,\forall m\,,

(388)

with
\theta
a single real constant. This concludes the proof.
